Now: no purge of the vault or its secrets; the deleted vault keeps its name for its 7-day retention; a rebuild in that time recovers it (`recover_soft_deleted_key_vaults`, the provider's default; not yet seen) or uses a new `KEY_VAULT_NAME`; the lead can purge by hand (`docs/operations/runbooks/dev-environment-teardown.md`). No IAM change. Not verified: the recovery path, and that the destroy succeeds with these settings (the first teardown).
Update 2026-11-20 (the first teardown): the destroy succeeded with no purge; the vault is soft-deleted, scheduled purge 2026-11-27. Still not verified: a rebuild recovering it.

**109. Cosmos access uses a key through Key Vault; managed identity is a later hardening item**
Type: Deferred hardening · Source: `WI-DEP-001`, ADR-0007, ADR-0020 (2026-10-31) · Hat: Security, Architect
Status: Deferred · approved by the lead on 2026-10-31 to do later; trigger: after the first successful deploy
The services read only `Cosmos:ConnectionString`. The dev stack therefore holds the account key as a Key Vault secret set after apply (never in state). Update 2026-11-20 (item 214): "never in state" was not accurate. The Key Vault secret's value is now kept out of state (write-only `value_wo`), but the Cosmos account resource records the account's keys and connection strings in state on every refresh; this item is what removes their use. The lead accepted the keys in the dev state until this item is done, with the state store's access control as the control (ADR-0020 note of 2026-11-20). The hardening is to let the services authenticate to Cosmos with their managed identity and a data-plane role (ADR-0007), which removes the secret, the post-apply step and its Secrets Officer grant (item 108). It changes service data-access code and the Terraform role assignment, so it needs the lead's approval of the change when it is started. The local emulator cannot use Entra, so the local path keeps a key.

**110. Two runs can plan and then apply in turn, so an approval may not be for the plan that is applied**
Type: Design gap · Source: `WI-DEP-001`, ADR-0020 decision 6, gate G6 (2026-10-31) · Hat: Platform Engineering, Architect
Status: Open · decided 2026-10-31 (design); build waits for the first real plan and apply
ADR-0020 says the approval is for the exact plan applied. Azure's exclusive lock on an environment is checked after approvals, so two runs can plan against the same state, both wait for approval at the same time, and then apply one after the other; the second applies a plan made against a state that no longer exists. For the infrastructure apply, Terraform should refuse a stale saved plan, but that is documented behaviour, not seen in this pipeline. For the application deploy there is no saved plan, so two runs could deploy different image digests in either order. Not proved by the rehearsal (its steps change nothing; G6 now tests only the lock). Options, in order of preference: re-plan after approval and abort if the result differs from the approved plan (recommended for the infrastructure apply); accept Terraform's stale-plan refusal as the backstop and prove it with two overlapping real runs; run the plan stage under the same lock; allow one open run (the lock's default `runLatest` cancels older waiting runs). For the deploy: record the digest deployed and refuse an older build than the one running. Decision and build wait for the first real plan and apply. **Decided 2026-10-31 by the lead: for the infrastructure apply, re-plan after approval and abort if the result differs from the approved plan;** Terraform's stale-plan refusal stays as the second layer and is proved with two overlapping real runs. The application deploy records the digest it deployed and refuses an older build than the one running (to be designed with the deploy stage).
Update 2026-11-20: built for the infrastructure apply and the teardown, not yet run. After the approval, `bakery-deploy` re-plans under the lock in the same step that applies, compares the plan with the approved value-free summary (`scripts/ci/plan-summary.mjs --expect`: the same resources, actions and changed outputs) and refuses on any difference; the saved plan it applies is its own and never leaves that step. Unit-tested, and the pipeline structure tests fail if the re-plan, the check or their order is removed (checked by mutation). Left: the first real apply, two overlapping real runs (Terraform's stale-plan refusal), and the application deploy's digest rule.

**111. Twelve whitespace-format errors in the Auth tests on `main`**
Type: Quality gate · Source: found 2026-11-01 while running `dotnet format whitespace tools/BakeryMarketplace.slnx --verify-no-changes` · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (checked: `dotnet format whitespace` is clean on both solutions, so the 12 whitespace errors were fixed some time after 2026-11-01. The check on `tools/BakeryMarketplace.slnx` also found three import-order errors under `dotnet format style` in `ClientCredentialsTokenProviderTests.cs`, `CorrelationIdMiddlewareTests.cs` and `SecretValidatorTests.cs`; fixed, whitespace and style are now clean on both solutions and the tools solution builds. The pipeline's format step runs only on the root solution `BakeryMarketplace.slnx`, so the tools solution's style is checked locally; it is in `CLAUDE.md`'s command list)
The check reports 12 whitespace errors in `tools/tests/BakeryMarketplace.Auth.Tests/ServiceAuthIntegrationTests.cs` (line 129 and nearby), present on `main` before this branch's changes (checked by stashing them). The root solution check (`BakeryMarketplace.slnx`) was clean. Fix with `dotnet format whitespace tools/BakeryMarketplace.slnx`, then confirm no other file changes; check whether the pipeline runs the `tools/` solution check.

**112. A Redis subscriber whose handler fails never gets the message again, and it is never dead-lettered**
Type: Latent defect · Source: found 2026-11-01 reading `RedisStreamsEventTransport` for `N-03` · Hat: Platform Engineering, Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-01
Resolution: the subscription loop now reads the group's pending entries as well as new messages. A message idle for `redeliverAfter` (30 seconds by default, a constructor parameter) is claimed and the handler runs again, with the delivery count taken from Redis; at `maxDeliveryAttempts` it is copied to `events:{type}:dead-letter` and acknowledged. A consumer that died leaves its pending messages for the others to claim. Shown against the local Redis with a handler that fails once (delivered again, succeeds, nothing left pending) and one that always fails (dead-lettered after 3 deliveries with the attempt count and envelope). Not covered: ordering across a redelivery (a redelivered message can arrive after later ones), so handlers must tolerate out-of-order events, as the vendor-status handler does with its sequence check; and the dead-letter stream has no consumer or alert.
Original text: Found by reading the code, not yet shown by a test. The subscription loop reads only new messages (`StreamPosition.NewMessages`) and keeps its attempt counts in memory, keyed by entry id. When a handler throws, the message is not acknowledged, but nothing ever reads the group's pending entries again, so the attempt count never reaches `maxDeliveryAttempts`, the dead-letter stream is never written, and the message stays pending for ever. The loop's own comments and the "dead-letter on max retries" claim in the class summary therefore do not hold for a handler failure. It does not matter while the only subscriber (catalog's vendor status) can lose an event harmlessly, but a read model (`N-03`) would silently miss an update. Fix: on start and periodically, read the group's pending entries (`XPENDING`/`XAUTOCLAIM`) and redeliver them, counting deliveries from the stream's own delivery count rather than memory; add a contract test with a handler that fails once. Needs the shared library changed, so it waits for approval with the `N-03` plan.

**113. The vendor order view needs names, and must say what still costs a read per order**
Type: Decision and requirement · Source: lead, 2026-11-01, while planning the vendor order detail after `N-03` · Hat: Architect, Product, Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-01 (built; see the limits below)
Decided by the lead 2026-11-01: order events carry **ids** (listing ids, modifier ids; the pickup window and instructions on `OrderPlaced`), not names, because the catalog owns the names. The vendor view shows names by keeping a **cache of listing and modifier names with a periodic refresh**, not by reading the catalog for every order shown. Requirement for whoever builds it: when the vendor view is built, **record anything that still causes a read per order** (any lookup that is not a point read of the vendor read model) and what it costs, in this item or its successor. **A cache miss is a separate concern and does not count as a per-order read** (lead, 2026-11-01): a miss may trigger a refresh of the cache, but orders that arrive together must share **one** refresh, not one each (a single-flight refresh), and that is what to build and test. Until then a vendor sees ids. Open design points: where the cache lives (in the ordering service, fed by the catalog's `ListingPublished` events or by a periodic read of the vendor's listings), how long a name may be stale, what is shown when a listing was renamed or removed after the order, and whether the cache is per vendor. Related: item 76 (the read model), and the 2026-11-01 finding that a vendor has no order detail read at all.
Resolution: `ListingNameCache` (ordering service, `Checkout/ListingNameCache.cs`) holds each listing's name and its modifiers' names, read through the catalog's public listing read (`ICatalogGateway.GetListingAsync`, whose `CatalogModifier` now carries the name). The vendor list and the vendor order read enrich every line with `listingName` and `modifiers[{id, name}]`; a name is null when it is not known, and the ids are always there. **Misses:** a listing not held is fetched once, and every request waiting for that listing while it is fetched shares that one fetch (shown with 50 concurrent callers and one read); a listing the catalog does not have is remembered as unknown; a failed fetch is not retried for 30 seconds, and the list still answers with ids. **Refresh:** `ListingNameRefresher` runs every 10 minutes (`ListingNames:RefreshMinutes`) and fetches again every entry that old; an entry nobody has asked for in 24 hours is dropped; the cache holds at most 2000 listings; an entry the refresh has failed to renew for an hour is no longer served. **Reads, as the lead asked to have recorded:** the vendor list and the vendor order read cost **no read per order**: names cost at most one catalog read per distinct listing that is not held (three orders of one listing, one read; the next lists and reads, none), plus one read per held listing every 10 minutes. The two point reads of the read model are the only reads per request. A vendor *action* (confirm, decline and so on) still costs two point reads (the read model, then the order) and falls back to the old query across customers when the read model does not have the order yet; that is per action, not per order shown. Checkout still reads each listing once per line for its own price check; that is the customer path and is not changed here. Shown with 9 tests in memory and through the real HTTP gateway against a fake catalog server; not run against the real catalog.
Limits: (1) the cache is per ordering-service instance, so each instance fetches and refreshes for itself; (2) the public listing read answers only for Published listings of verified vendors, so an order for a listing that was later unpublished, deleted or whose vendor was suspended shows ids, not names: showing names for those needs a service-role read of a listing by id whatever its status, an authenticated catalog endpoint that is an auth decision for the lead; (3) a page of orders that touch many distinct listings makes one read for each on a cold cache, because the catalog has no read of several listings at once; (4) a rename shows after at most one refresh interval (10 minutes); (5) the cache stores no per-vendor data, only public listing names.

**114. A customer cannot send the vendor any free text, and pickup instructions are never set**
Type: Product gap · Source: found 2026-11-01 reading checkout for the vendor order view · Hat: Product, Engineering/Build
Status: Open
Checkout accepts a vendor, line items (listing, chosen modifiers, quantity) and an optional pickup window; `FulfillmentDetails.ForPickup` is called with the window only, so the `Instructions` field that the order, its events and the vendor read model all carry is never filled. The only customization a customer can express is a modifier chosen from the listing. A vendor therefore cannot be told "no nuts, please" or "ring the side bell". If free text is wanted it needs a limit, validation and display rules (it is customer-written text shown to a vendor), a decision on whether it counts as a customization for the auto-confirm rule (`AutoConfirmPolicy`), and the checkout request, BFF schema and web app to carry it. Not built; nothing in the vendor view depends on it.

**115. The login, Relay and proxy code is copied between the two web apps**
Type: Tech debt · Source: `WI-QUE-002`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Engineering/Build, Architect
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02
Resolution: the login, session and GraphQL proxy code exists once, in `packages/web-session` (a `file:` dependency, not an npm workspace), and both web apps use it; the copies in `apps/web-customer` and `apps/web-queue-display` were deleted. The remaining copy is only the thin session client each app has (`src/lib/session.ts`: who is logged in, log in, log out) and its three-line API routes. The original text follows.
`apps/web-queue-display` has a copy of `apps/web-customer`'s browser login (`src/lib/auth/`: PKCE, session, claims, config, login, browser, return-to), the GraphQL proxy route (`src/lib/graphql-proxy.ts`, `pages/api/graphql.ts`, `pages/api/config.ts`), `RequireLogin`, and their unit tests (about 600 lines). The lead chose copying for now (2026-11-02) to avoid refactoring web-customer. The cost is that a fix to the login (a security fix above all) has to be made twice, and the copies will drift. Only two things differ on purpose: the default client id (`web-queue-display`) and a Relay environment with no server rendering. Remedy: an npm workspace package (for example `packages/web-auth`) used by both apps, extracted with the existing tests moved to it and both apps' suites run before and after; a refactor that needs the lead's approval. Until then, any change under `src/lib/auth/` in one app must be made in the other.

**116. The queue display cannot stay logged in on a wall screen, and has not met the real issuer**
Type: Design gap, auth decision (human approval required) · Source: `WI-QUE-002`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Architect, Security
Status: Open · partly resolved 2026-11-02
Progress 2026-11-02: (1) the wall-screen problem is fixed by ADR-0019: the session is on the server, a reload keeps it, the token is renewed there, and a session lasts until its idle limit (12 hours) or absolute lifetime (7 days). (2) The issuer settings are no longer needed: the mock issuer registers `http://localhost:3100/api/auth/callback` (and port 3000's) by default, and the browser makes no cross-origin call to it. Still open: a run of the queue display against the real stack and issuer (`WI-AUTH-007` phase 4), and what a wall screen does when the idle limit passes (it shows "log in again", as designed). The original text follows.
Progress: ADR-0019 (a server-side session behind an HttpOnly cookie, renewal on the server) was Accepted 2026-11-02 with the lead's approval and decisions; `WI-AUTH-007` builds it and will resolve items 24 and 115 and simplify the stream authentication of ADR-0021. Phase 1 (the mock issuer's refresh grant) is built.
Two things. (1) The login is the customer app's: authorization code with PKCE in the browser, the access token in memory only (item 24). A kitchen or counter screen is usually unattended, has no keyboard, and reloads or sleeps; every reload logs it out and the token expires in an hour, so someone has to log in again, in a browser, several times a day. The remedies are all auth design and need the lead: the cookie session of `N-01` (item 24), a long-lived device credential for the screen scoped to reading one vendor's queue, or a refresh token. Until one is chosen the screen shows "log in again" when the token ends (tested). (2) It has only been run against an issuer stubbed inside the browser. To use the real mock issuer the issuer must allow the app's origin and callback: `MockOidc__RedirectUris__1=http://localhost:3100/auth/callback` and `MockOidc__AllowedOrigins__1=http://localhost:3100` on the `mock-oidc` service (the defaults allow only port 3000). That is a change to the issuer's configuration, so it is left to the lead; after it, log in as the vendor `vendor-shop-001` and run the journey against the real stack.

**117. The catalog tests could deadlock on a machine with few cores (found as a 38-minute run on Azure)**
Type: Test defect, found by a pipeline run · Source: the pull request for `feature/stock-outbox-hardening`, 2026-11-01 · Hat: Engineering/Build, Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02
Resolution: the `Test catalog-service` step ran for 38 minutes on the Azure agent (2 cores, one parallel job) and was cancelled by the lead; the same tests take 4 seconds on a laptop and passed in the repo's Linux check. The log stopped after the skipped Cosmos tests. Cause (reproduced with `DOTNET_PROCESSOR_COUNT=2`, and by blame-hang with the stuck test named): xUnit v2 runs tests on one worker thread per core and resumes awaited work on those threads. Test constructors called `.GetAwaiter().GetResult()` on calls that begin with `await Task.Yield()` (the in-memory repository and the event publisher do), so a worker was blocked waiting for work that needed a worker. With enough cores there is always a free one. `main` already hung with one worker thread (`StockReservationTests`, `Verify...GetResult()` in the constructor); the classes added on `feature/stock-outbox-hardening` (`OwnListingsApiTests`, `ReservationRetentionTests`, `ListingOutboxTests`) used the same pattern and made two threads enough to hang. It was not a slow or large test. Fix: set-up moved to `IAsyncLifetime.InitializeAsync` (or an async factory) in those four places, and one `.Result` in `CheckoutApiTests`; both services' tests pass with 1 and 2 worker threads. Guards: `scripts/ci/tests/no-blocking-in-dotnet-tests.test.mjs` (in `node-gates --tests`) fails on `.GetAwaiter().GetResult()`, `.Result`, `.Wait()` in any .NET test file; `linux-check.mjs` now runs its containers with 2 CPUs and `--blame-hang-timeout 3m`; the pipeline's three `dotnet test` steps have `--blame-hang-timeout 5m`, so a real hang fails with a dump in five minutes instead of running to the job timeout. Checked: the old code fails the 2-CPU Linux check after 3 minutes ("Test Run Aborted"). Not yet seen on Azure.

**118. Sessions live in an LRU cache Redis, so a full cache ends logins**
Type: Known limitation · Source: `WI-AUTH-007` phase 3, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Platform Engineering, Architect
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 · built: a `redis-sessions` instance with `noeviction` (compose profile `with-redis`, port 6381; dev Terraform container app `redis-sessions`, TCP 6381) holds the web apps' sessions; the cache Redis is unchanged; Terraform tests assert noeviction, the port and the web app's address; ADR-0019 has a dated amendment. Not applied on Azure (item 119). No alert on a full store yet. `redis-cache` in the dev Terraform is now used by nothing (drop it or keep it for ADR-0002: the lead decides). Decision was: a separate `redis-sessions` instance with `noeviction` (eviction policy is per instance, so a key prefix or numbered database in the cache Redis cannot be protected from `allkeys-lru`) in `docker-compose.yml` and the dev Terraform
The lead chose the cache Redis for the session store (ADR-0019). The cache Redis is configured `--maxmemory 128mb --maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru` (`docker-compose.yml`, and the same in the dev Terraform's `redis-cache`), which evicts the least recently used keys when memory is full, session records included: an evicted session is a user (or a wall screen) that is suddenly logged out. At this scale 128 MB holds hundreds of thousands of sessions (a record is about 1.5 KB), so it will not happen in a demo, but it is the wrong failure mode for a session store and nothing alerts on it. Options: a separate `redis-sessions` instance with `noeviction` (a full store then refuses new logins loudly instead of dropping old ones); or keep the shared instance and alert on evicted keys (`evicted_keys` in `INFO stats`). The session package keeps its keys under `web-session:<client id>:` so either is a configuration change. No code change is needed to decide; the decision is the lead's.

**119. The dev Terraform now expects values nobody has set**
Type: Deployment follow-up · Source: `WI-AUTH-007` phase 3, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Open · stays open until the first Azure apply (decided 2026-11-06: a checklist, linked from the `WI-DEP-001` apply runbook). Added 2026-11-06 (item 118): the first apply also creates `redis-sessions` on TCP port 6381, and the web app points at it. Added 2026-11-06 (item 137): decide what to set as `BFF_TRUSTED_CLIENT_SOURCES` on the BFF container app (the web app's address range in the Container Apps environment); until then the BFF counts per connection.
Nothing has been planned or applied on Azure. When the dev environment is next applied: (1) the Key Vault secret `session-encryption-key` is created as a placeholder, and its value (32 random bytes, base64url) must be set out of band before the web app can log anyone in (the web routes answer 503 "Login is not configured" until then); (2) a new `redis-cache` container app (TCP port 6380) holds the sessions; (3) the mock issuer's registered callback is `<web>/api/auth/callback` and its browser CORS origin setting is gone. The mocked-provider tests cover the shape (`infra/environments/dev/tests/dev.tftest.hcl`), not the behaviour on Azure. The first real apply should record the login working end to end (a browser, the deployed web app, the deployed mock issuer, the session in `redis-cache`).

**120. The mock issuer skips the refresh token's client check when `client_id` is omitted**
Type: Hardening · Source: security review of `WI-AUTH-007`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 · built (`MockOidcIssuer.RefreshTokenGrant`; test `ARefreshWithoutAClientIdIsRefusedAndDoesNotSpendTheToken`, shown to fail before the change; applies to the deployed dev mock issuer on its next image build). Approved as: `client_id` required on the refresh grant, a mismatch or missing value refused, test in `RefreshTokenGrantTests` (shown to fail without the check)
In `MockOidcIssuer.RefreshTokenGrant` the check that a refresh token is used by the client it was issued to runs only when the request carries a `client_id`; leaving it out skips the check. A stolen, still unused refresh token could be presented without naming a client. Exploiting it needs the token first, and the mock issuer is a stand-in, but it is deployed to the dev environment (`WI-DEP-001`). Fix: require `client_id` on the refresh grant and refuse a mismatch or a missing value, with a test in `RefreshTokenGrantTests`. Auth logic, so it needs the lead's approval before the change.

**121. The code exchange does not validate `iss` or `aud` on the token it receives**
Type: Hardening · Source: security review of `WI-AUTH-007`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Architect
Status: Open · deferred 2026-11-06 to the move to a real identity provider (the lead agreed)
`packages/web-session` takes the access token from the token endpoint over the server's own call and reads its claims without checking the issuer or audience (`claims.ts` says so; every service still validates the token itself, ADR-0006). That is safe while the issuer is the mock, reached directly. When a real identity provider replaces it, check `iss` against `AUTH_ISSUER` and `aud` against the expected audience before a session is created, and verify the signature if the token is ever used for more than display. Do it with the move to a real provider, not before.

**122. Nothing stops `SESSION_ALLOW_MEMORY_STORE=1` in a deployed environment**
Type: Hardening · Source: security review of `WI-AUTH-007`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 · built: `packages/web-session` refuses `SESSION_REDIS_URL=memory` unless `APP_ORIGIN` is loopback, even with `SESSION_ALLOW_MEMORY_STORE=1` (test in `foundations.test.ts`, fails without the change); a dev Terraform test asserts the web app has neither setting (a guard: it passed before the change too). Approved as: a Terraform test that the deployed web app has neither setting, and a refusal in `packages/web-session` when `APP_ORIGIN` is not loopback
`SESSION_REDIS_URL=memory` is accepted only with `SESSION_ALLOW_MEMORY_STORE=1`, which is a guard against a mistake, not a block. If it were set on the deployed web app, sessions would live in one process, be lost on restart and not be shared between instances. Fix: a test in `infra/environments/dev/tests/dev.tftest.hcl` that the web app's environment does not contain either setting, and optionally a refusal in the package when `APP_ORIGIN` is not loopback.

**123. Review and strengthen the session closeout and session start handoff**
Type: Process · Source: lead, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Process, Project Management
Status: Open
At the start of the 2026-11-02 session the AI agent's memory said the vendor-order-detail PR was open and `WI-AUTH-007` waiting on the lead; in fact the PR was already merged (rebase-merge, branch deleted), `WI-AUTH-007` phases 1 to 4 and the first-pass security review were on `main`, and `docs/product/backlog/current-plan.md` still listed the vendor apps as not built. The closeout wrote a snapshot of the end of the work, not a check of it, and the start of the session trusted that snapshot. To do together: (1) a closeout checklist run at the end of a session: refresh the memory file, the status banners, `current-plan.md` and the register, record the commit and branch state, and mark anything pending on the human as "as of <time>"; (2) a start-of-session routine that verifies before it reports: `git fetch --prune`, compare local and `origin/main` by patch (`git cherry`), read the banners of the work items named in memory, and list what differs from the memory; (3) decide where each kind of state lives (memory for the lead's preferences and pointers, `current-plan.md` for focus, banners for status) so the same fact is not kept in three places; (4) consider a `/closeout` and `/resume` skill or a script in `scripts/dev/`. Related: `docs/method/context-completeness.md`, `docs/method/lessons.md`.
What the 2026-11-02 session showed (added at its close): the start-of-session check worked once it was asked for (a fetch, a comparison of local and remote by patch, reading the banners of the work items named in memory) and it found the stale memory in a few minutes; a closeout done in one pass at the end then kept the record true: the work item banner and notes, the register, `current-plan.md`, the memory file and its index line, the usage record, and the test counts in `CLAUDE.md`. The same pass found two numbers that were wrong in the draft (the count of unpushed commits, the number of wasted runs), so a closeout should recount from the repository and the logs and not from memory. Candidate checklist for the closeout, from what was actually done: banners and notes of the items touched; the register (new, resolved, changed); `current-plan.md` (what is built, what is next, what waits on the lead); the memory file for the project and its index line; the usage record when the lead has pasted the screen; counts in `CLAUDE.md` and `test-strategy.md` regenerated, not typed; `git log origin/main..HEAD` and `git status` for what is and is not pushed.

**124. The web apps' JSON GraphQL route reads a request body without a size limit**
Type: Hardening · Source: step 5 of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02
Resolution: `proxyGraphql` now reads at most `MAX_REQUEST_BODY_BYTES` (64 KB, shared with the stream route) and answers 413 above it, with a test in `packages/web-session/tests/graphql-proxy.test.ts`; approved by the lead the same day.
`proxyGraphql` in `packages/web-session/src/graphql-proxy.ts` reads the whole request body into memory (`readBody`) because the framework's body parser is off on `/api/graphql`. Anyone who can reach the app, logged in or not, can post a very large body and make the server hold it. The new subscription route (`/api/graphql-stream`) limits its body to 64 KB and answers 413 above it; the BFF limits its own to 64 KB too, so the JSON route is the one without a limit. Fix: the same limit in `proxyGraphql` (a checkout body is a few hundred bytes), with a test, and a note in `web-session`'s README.

**125. A hung Redis can hold up every stream open or close on an ordering instance**
Type: Hardening · Source: security review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02
Resolution: `VendorOrderStreamHub` keeps one lock per vendor (a `SemaphoreSlim` in each vendor's entry) and counts the instance's streams with an atomic reserve-and-release instead of under a lock, so a slow Redis subscribe delays only that vendor's own opens and closes; the guarantees of the single lock are kept (one subscription per vendor, the limits, no stream left on a subscription being disposed). Tests in `VendorOrderStreamHubTests.cs`: a stuck subscribe for one vendor does not delay another vendor's open or close (fails on the old hub), 40 opens at once for one vendor leave the limit, one subscription and an exact count, 60 opens at once never pass the instance limit and give back the places they were refused, 200 rounds of a close racing an open never leave the new stream on a disposed subscription, and a failed subscribe leaves nothing behind. Approved by the lead the same day.
`VendorOrderStreamHub.OpenAsync` and `ReleaseAsync` take one gate, and `OpenAsync` waits for the Redis subscription inside it (`RedisBroadcast.SubscribeAsync`, which has no timeout of its own beyond the client library's default of about 5 seconds). With Redis unreachable that is quick to fail and the endpoint answers 503, but with Redis slow or half-open every other vendor's open, and every close, queues behind it for that time. Fix: subscribe outside the gate (create the vendor's entry first, with the subscription as a task the later openers await), or give the subscribe its own short timeout; with a test that a slow subscribe for one vendor does not delay another's.
What the lock is for (asked at the review, 2026-11-02): `_gate` in `VendorOrderStreamHub` is the one lock of the hub's bookkeeping. It makes four things atomic that must not interleave: finding or creating a vendor's single broadcast subscription on this instance; counting streams against the per-vendor (5) and per-instance (500) limits and retiring the oldest; adding or removing a stream from the vendor's list; and unsubscribing when the last stream goes, so that a stream opening at that moment cannot end up with a subscription that is being disposed. It is correct; the problem is only that the Redis subscribe, a network call, runs while it is held. The fix keeps the guarantees with a lock per vendor instead of one for all, and the subscribe awaited inside only that vendor's lock.

**126. The web app's stream route counts a place before it has read the request, and has no limit per session**
Type: Hardening · Source: security review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02
Resolution: `createGraphqlStreamHandler` now reads the body before it counts the stream (a slow body holds a connection, which the server's timeouts handle, but no place), and a login may have at most 5 streams open (`maxPerSession`; a sixth is a 429 with `Retry-After`), alongside the 500 per process. Tests in `packages/web-session/tests/graphql-stream.test.ts` (the slow body and the per-session cap), each checked to fail when the fix is taken out. Approved by the lead the same day.
`createGraphqlStreamHandler` (`packages/web-session/src/handlers.ts`) increments its count of open streams before `proxyGraphqlStream` reads the request body, and limits only the process (500), not one session. A logged-in user can therefore send slow bodies and hold places, so that other screens on the same server get 503 and fall back to polling (degraded, not down; ordering itself caps a vendor at 5 streams, and the BFF's equivalent was fixed in the same review by counting only when a subscription is established). Fix: read the body first, count after, and cap per session (for example 5, like ordering); with tests.

**127. Order changes travel on Redis Pub/Sub in the clear, readable by anything that can reach the events Redis**
Type: Residual risk · Source: security review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Architect
Status: Open
The channel `vendor-orders:{vendorId}` carries the vendor read model document as JSON, including the customer's id (the stream endpoint removes it before sending to the vendor). Redis has no per-channel access control worth relying on, so anyone with a connection to the events Redis can subscribe to every vendor's channel. The event streams on the same Redis already carry the same data (the order events name the customer), so this adds no new exposure; but it should be written down for the deployed design: the events Redis must require authentication and be reachable only from the services (ADR-0007, the Terraform for the deployed environment), and the document could drop the customer id before it is published (the stream does not need it). Decide when the Redis for the deployed environment is built.

**128. Nothing limits the rate of connections, logins or requests per caller, and nothing tests that it holds**
Type: Gap · Source: the lead's questions at the review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Architect, Security
Status: Open · first step built 2026-11-02 on `feature/rate-limiting` (`WI-RATE-001`, In review); what remains is listed after the description
What exists: item 27 (per-user throttling at the BFF, an open question), the epic `EPIC-RATE-LIMITING` (`RATE-001` to `RATE-003` not started; `RATE-004` and `GW-006` are only the coarse per-subscription policy at API Management, which cannot limit per user, ADR-0008), and nothing built. `WI-QUE-003` added limits on what one connection or one login may hold (streams per vendor 5, per login 5, per process and per BFF 500, body 64 KB, a write timeout, a silence limit) but nothing limits how fast a caller may open them: anyone can post to the BFF's event-stream route or the web app's routes as fast as the network allows (a junk bearer token costs the BFF one call to ordering, which answers 401; each login attempt costs a call to the issuer), and the stream can be opened again and again. "Reconciled" means: decide where per-address and per-login limits live (API Management cannot per user, so the web app's server and the BFF), set numbers, build the first cheap step (a token bucket per address on `/api/auth/*`, `/api/graphql`, `/api/graphql-stream` and the BFF), and test that a burst is refused and normal use is not. Link: items 27 and 126, `RATE-001`.

**Built (`WI-RATE-001`, decided by the lead 2026-11-02):** an in-memory token bucket per key (bounded to 10,000 keys) in `packages/web-session`, used by both web apps: logins 10 a minute per address (start and callback each), `/api/graphql` 120 a minute per login (per address without one), stream opens 10 a minute per login, and an address ceiling of 600 a minute across the other routes checked before the session store is asked; the BFF has a backstop of 600 a minute per source address. The address is the connection's own unless `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` says how many proxies add to `X-Forwarded-For`. **Still open:** a shared (Redis) limiter for more than one replica; the production numbers and hop count (with `WI-DEP-001`); per-action limits for vendors; limits at the services themselves; a load test that approaches the limits (item 129).

**129. No load, soak, fuzz or penetration testing is planned or recorded**
Type: Gap · Source: the lead's questions at the review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Quality, Security
Status: Resolved 2026-11-03 for the local stack as `WI-SEC-005` (In review): the threat model, the fuzz and property tests, the 66-minute soak, the ZAP scan and the review statement are written and recorded; what each shows and does not show is in the evidence files. Findings from them are items 134 to 137 (134, 135 and 136 fixed; 137 open). An independent review has not happened.
What the docs say today: `test-strategy.md` lists load and capacity as "deliberately not tested", `WI-QA-002` (In progress) has throughput among its remaining rows, and `nfrs.md` says nothing has been load-tested. There is no item for: **load and soak** (the live queue has limits, 500 streams per process, that have never been approached; a stream lives up to an hour and renews; many screens reconnecting together after a restart has not been tried; the delay was measured with one screen and a light load); **fuzzing** (the parsers and inputs added or exposed by this work: the BFF's reader of ordering's event stream, the BFF's event-stream and JSON routes, the web app's routes and headers, the GraphQL inputs); **a threat model** (none exists for the browser, session and stream surface; none for the services); **penetration or dynamic scanning** (`WI-SEC-004` is Deferred and covers pipeline security checks and Terraform validation, not testing the running system); **independent review** (every security review so far, `WI-AUTH-007` and `WI-QUE-003`, is a read by the AI agent that wrote the code; the lead's review is the only other). "Reconciled" means: decide the level that fits a portfolio project and write it down. Suggested minimum: a load and soak check of the stream (the delay script with many screens, run for an hour with renewals) under `WI-QA-002`; property and fuzz tests for the event-stream reader and the BFF's inputs (cheap with the Node test runner); a one-page threat model of the browser, session and stream surface; an OWASP ZAP baseline scan of the local stack added to `WI-SEC-004`'s scope; and a plain statement in the repository of what has and has not been independently reviewed (before the repository is public, item 93).

**130. Revisit the NFRs, the Definitions of Ready and Done, the test strategy and the working pattern, to close the gaps this work showed**
Type: Process · Source: the lead's request at the review of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Process, Architect, Quality
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead closed it; the draft was merged to `main` as written and its proposals are in the documents named below, none further tracked here) · drafted 2026-11-02 on `feature/process-review` for the lead's review: `docs/process/verification-policy.md` (new: change classes V0 to V3 and J, inline checks, scheduled checkpoints with triggers and exit conditions, evidence records, the end of the quality track), proposed additions to the Definitions of Ready and Done, the work item template, `test-strategy.md`, `nfrs.md` (3.8, 3.9, 5.1, 5.2) and the lessons record (L-21 to L-23). Nothing is adopted until the lead chooses; the numbers marked proposed are the lead's to set
Findings to take to the lead as proposed edits (none made yet, except the first): (1) **NFR 3.3 was out of date** for the days between the cookie session being built and 2026-11-02: it said CSRF was "not applicable" on a condition that the work itself ended. Corrected. The cause is that nothing ties a work item to the NFRs and ADRs it affects; the Definition of Done says "docs affected are updated" but the NFRs are not in any item's list. Proposal: an "ADRs and NFRs affected" line in the work item template and in the Definition of Done. (2) **The NFRs have no section for long-lived connections and real-time delivery** (a latency target: the push measured 68 ms p50 and 99 ms p95 with no target to compare to; connection and per-caller limits; behaviour when a client is slow or gone), none for abuse resistance (item 128), and none for observability beyond hygiene (3.6: the stream's metrics exist, nothing requires or reads them). (3) **Security verification has no level.** The Definition of Done says "reviewed in a separate pass"; for security-sensitive items that was the author's own read, and it found real flaws late (the BFF counted a stream before reading its body, found after five steps were built; the web route had the same flaw and no per-session limit; a client that stops reading held a stream open). Proposal: for items that touch auth or hold connections, an adversarial review pass is a step before building the sensitive part and again before "In review", and the Definition of Ready asks for the abuse cases (a slow client, many clients, a large input, a client that never reads, cleanup when the client leaves) as acceptance criteria. (4) **Definition of Done additions**: a limits checklist for network-facing code (size, count, time, rate, per caller, cleanup); tests that guard a security property are shown to fail when the property is taken out; a new dependency has a review record (done by hand for `graphql-sse`); a cross-service feature has an evidence run on the real stack with its starting state stated. (5) **The test strategy's numbers are stale** (BFF 75, ordering 337 and 3 skipped, skeleton 21, web 53) because they are typed by hand; they should come from a script (`WI-QA-002`). (6) **Working pattern**: chain commands with `&&` so a failed edit cannot be followed by a long run (it cost three runs here); do not keep the Docker state between runs (`--keep`); approval per security-sensitive step, recorded in the spec, worked and should stay; memory goes stale between sessions (item 123). "Reconciled" means: the lead chooses which of these to adopt, the edits to `nfrs.md`, `definition-of-ready.md`, `definition-of-done.md`, `test-strategy.md` and the work item template are drafted for review in a branch of their own, and items 128 and 129 are planned.

**131. WI-QUE-003 is Done with these decisions and verifications still open**
Type: Open decisions and verification · Source: the close of `WI-QUE-003`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Project Management, Quality
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 · the lead decided all four: (1) `WI-QUE-003` stays Done (reopen only if the first Azure run of its jobs fails); (2) item 127 stays open on its own and is revisited when the deployed Redis is designed; (3) ADR-0021 decision 4 stays unchecked until `WI-DEP-001` gives a cloud path; (4) the delay display is accepted as it is (quote the request-based figures). The "Verification not done" list below is not revisited until its trigger (the first Azure run, a cloud path, a real issuer) exists.
Decisions for the lead (asked at the close; answered 2026-11-06 as above): (1) **Done or Built.** `status-and-supersession.md` says Done needs "a clean-agent or CI run where it applies". The .NET tests ran on Linux in a 2-CPU container (`linux-check.mjs`) and the real stack ran with host processes and with containers, but the Azure pipeline has not run these changes. The item was marked Done on the lead's approval; if the strict reading is wanted, change it to Built with "the first Azure pipeline run" as the missing verification. (2) **Item 127** (order changes cross Redis Pub/Sub in the clear) stays open until the deployed Redis is designed: confirm that is acceptable. (3) **ADR-0021 decision 4** (a streamed response through API Management and Container Apps ingress) stays unchecked until a cloud path exists: confirm that is acceptable. (4) **The delay display** (`?delay=1`, and the "event to screen" figure) is valid only when the services and the screen share a clock; in containers on Docker Desktop it read about a second too high (an ADR-0021 note says so). Confirm that is acceptable, or ask for a measure that does not depend on it (for example the request-based figures only).
Verification not done, to do when the means exist: the first Azure pipeline run of the `queue`, `session` and skeleton jobs with these changes (it happens when the pull request is opened); a real issuer's token expiring on an open stream (the mock issuer's tokens last an hour and the lifetime is not configurable; a configurable lifetime in the mock issuer would let this be run); a service or Redis restarted under a live screen; an OTLP collector receiving the stream metrics; a browser other than Chromium; a streamed response through API Management and Container Apps ingress. Product work left: the vendor's actions (confirm, start, ready, complete) in the queue display, for which the endpoints exist (`ORD-007`).

**132. The mock-backed browser suites fail in Firefox and WebKit in 13 places that are not yet diagnosed**
Type: Verification gap · Source: the first cross-browser run, 2026-11-02 (`docs/evidence/cross-browser/mock-backed-2026-11-02.md`) · Hat: Quality, Frontend
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead closed it, accepting what is left as limits: only Chromium is supported and tested; the clean full `BROWSERS=all` repeat was not run, and the pipeline does not run Firefox or WebKit; the suites still take `BROWSERS=all` for anyone who wants the run) · diagnosed 2026-11-06 (`docs/evidence/cross-browser/diagnosis-2026-11-06.md`): of the 5 distinct failures, 4 are test flaws (the Firefox reload warning; the stock and order tests that take a URL change for readiness) and 1 is Playwright's WebKit reporting `sameSite` as `None`; no app defect. Fixed 2026-11-06 (test-only changes, `docs/evidence/cross-browser/mock-backed-2026-11-06.md`): the second full run found 663 of 665 and 296 of 300; its 6 failures (the WebKit cookie test, which cannot read the header on a redirect, and a cross-project clash of the queue display's rate-limit test addresses, found by that run) were fixed and re-verified one by one. Still open: the clean full `BROWSERS=all` repeat, scheduled as a checkpoint after the browser-facing items 137 and 150 are built (the lead's decision, so one run covers them), and whether the pipeline runs Firefox and WebKit. Until then only Chromium is supported and tested.
Both apps' suites now take `BROWSERS=all` (Chromium, Firefox, WebKit, Pixel 7, iPhone 14); the default stays Chromium so the pipeline is unchanged. Customer app: 309 of 320 passed; queue display: 158 of 160. The failures: one Firefox console warning (a script chunk failed to load on a listing page); the session cookie's `sameSite` read as `None` by WebKit where the server sends `Lax` (probably how Playwright's WebKit reports it, not confirmed); in WebKit the stock tests see the list still in the page after the URL changes, the order-refresh test sees two status requests where it expects one, and the "try again" test times out clicking "Refresh status". "Reconciled" means: each is diagnosed as a test flaw (fix the test), a reporting difference (adapt the assertion and say why), or an app defect (fix it, with approval if it touches the session), and the cross-browser run is repeated clean or with the remaining failures named. Nothing is run in a browser other than Chromium on the real stack, and the pipeline does not run the other browsers (a decision for the checkpoint's schedule, `verification-policy.md` section 4).

**133. The queue board replaces its list with a list read that may be older than a change already pushed**
Type: Known limitation (race) · Source: the first real-stack run of `WI-QUE-004`, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Frontend, Quality
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02 · the lead approved the fix; `mergeFetched` in `apps/web-queue-display/src/lib/queue.ts` (6 unit tests) is used by the board's `refresh`, and a browser test makes the list lag the stream (it fails with the merge taken out)
What was seen: the vendor clicked Accept; ordering confirmed the order and the stream pushed the new status; the board then asked for the list (the first version of the action did this), got an answer read from the vendor read model before it had caught up (still Placed, sequence 2), and `show()` replaced the board's newer card with the older one. The card went to Preparing and back to New, with its buttons enabled again, and stayed there: nothing else pushed. In `WI-QUE-004` the action no longer asks for the list after a success, which removes the case that made it likely, but the cause is in `QueueBoard`: `refresh()` (the first load, each poll, the 60-second check in Live mode, and a reconnect) puts the fetched list on the screen as it is, whatever the screen already holds from the stream. A list read that began before a push and ended after it can undo that push until the next push or poll. "Reconciled" means: the board merges a fetched list by sequence (for an order in both, the higher `sequence` wins; one in the list only is added; one on the screen only is kept if a push newer than the start of the fetch put it there), with unit tests for the merge in `queue.ts`; changing `WI-QUE-003`'s logic needs the lead's approval. The mock-backed tests cannot show it, because the mock's list and stream are always in step: it appeared only on the real stack.

**134. The web apps set no browser hardening headers: no Content-Security-Policy, no frame protection, no X-Content-Type-Options**
Type: Gap · Source: the threat model, 2026-11-02 (`docs/architecture/threat-model.md` E3 to E5) · Hat: Security, Frontend
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02 on `feature/quality-vendor-actions` (the lead asked for it): the headers are set in both apps' `next.config.js`, with browser tests of each header and of what they do (below). Two limits remain, listed after the original text.
Neither web app (nor the shared session package) sets `X-Frame-Options` or a CSP `frame-ancestors`, `Content-Security-Policy`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, or a general `Referrer-Policy` (only the login callback sets one); a search of the code finds none. Consequences: the queue display can be framed by another site, so a vendor could be tricked into pressing Accept or Decline (clickjacking; more serious since `WI-QUE-004`); and with no CSP a script that did run could call the API as the logged-in vendor (the page holds no token, so it cannot steal one, but the cookie still works for it). "Reconciled" means: set the headers in both apps' `next.config.js` (`headers()`): `frame-ancestors 'none'`, a CSP that Next.js 16 and the Relay client work under (a nonce or hashes for Next's inline scripts, to be tried and tested, report-only first), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, `Permissions-Policy` empty, and HSTS only for the deployed HTTPS origin; a browser test per header; the accessibility and e2e suites still pass. The lead decides whether this is part of `WI-SEC-005` or its own item.

**Done for 134:** `Content-Security-Policy` (`default-src 'self'`, `connect-src 'self'`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`, `object-src 'none'`, `base-uri 'self'`, `form-action 'self'`, images and fonts from the app and `data:`), `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin` (the login callback still sets `no-referrer`) and a `Permissions-Policy` that switches off the camera, microphone, location, payment and USB. Tests in both apps (`security-headers.spec.ts`, in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit and the two phone profiles): every kind of response carries them; another origin's page that frames the app gets no rendering of it (shown to fail with the frame headers removed); a script from another origin does not run and the browser reports the violation; a request from the page to another origin is blocked (shown to fail with `connect-src` opened). The whole mock-backed suites pass with the CSP on, and they fail on any console error, so no violation occurs in the flows they cover. **Still open:** `script-src` keeps `'unsafe-inline'` because Next.js writes inline scripts with no fixed hash, so an injected inline script is not stopped (nonces need a middleware and dynamic rendering of every page: a larger change, not started); `Strict-Transport-Security` is not set (the apps run over HTTP locally; it belongs on the HTTPS ingress of a deployed environment, `WI-DEP-001`); the real-stack run with the headers on is recorded in the evidence file once the soak has finished.

**135. The BFF puts no limit on the depth, alias count or cost of a GraphQL operation, and introspection is on**
Type: Gap · Source: the threat model, 2026-11-02 (D4) · Hat: Security, Architect
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02 on `feature/quality-vendor-actions` (the lead asked for it): limits on depth, root fields and total fields, as validation rules, and a switch to refuse introspection. See below.
The BFF validates operations with graphql-js's standard rules (`server.ts`); the body is limited to 64 KB, but within that an operation can nest deeply or repeat a field under many aliases, and each repeated field is a downstream call. Public queries (listings, pickup windows) need no token. Introspection is enabled, which is the default. "Reconciled" means: decide the limits (a maximum depth and a maximum number of aliases or fields; the schema is small, so low numbers fit), add them as a validation rule with tests (an operation over the limit is refused with a fixed message and no downstream call), decide whether introspection is wanted in a deployed environment, and add a fuzz case. Related: item 27 (per-user throttling) and `WI-RATE-001`.

**136. One request with `variables` that was not an object ended the BFF process (fixed 2026-11-02)**
Type: Defect (security: unauthenticated denial of service) · Source: the fuzz tests of `WI-SEC-005` step 2, 2026-11-02 · Hat: Security, Backend
Status: Resolved 2026-11-02 on `feature/quality-vendor-actions` (the lead is told in the summary; the fix is in the BFF's request handler, not in auth or the session package)
What was found: a POST to `/graphql` with `{"query":"{ __typename }","variables":"x"}` made graphql-js throw inside the BFF's async request handler; nothing caught it, so Node ended the process (reproduced on a standalone process: the next health check got no answer and the process exited with the unhandled error). No token is needed for the request. Because the web apps' `/api/graphql` route sends the request body on unchanged (by a reading of `graphql-proxy.ts`; not run end to end), the same request from a browser, anonymous, should have reached the BFF. It had existed since the BFF was written. A second, harmless defect of the same input class: a variable that cannot be coerced (an object for an `ID!`) was reported as `INTERNAL`, logged as a failed resolver, and so blamed the server for the caller's input.
Fixed: the handler answers 400 `BAD_REQUEST` when `variables` is present and not an object, or `operationName` is present and not a string (null counts as absent); anything the execution throws that is not an error of the operation is caught, logged by name only, and answered 500 with the generic error and the trace id; a coercion error (no path) is returned as it is, not as INTERNAL. Tests: `test/robustness.test.ts` starts the real process, sends the bodies, and checks it keeps serving (it fails without the fix); `test/fuzz.test.ts` sends 1,200 generated requests and 300 generated event-stream requests and requires no 5xx, no hang and no INTERNAL.
What this says about the project: nothing in the 124 earlier BFF tests, the browser suites or the real-stack runs sent a body like this, and the agent's own reviews of the BFF did not think of it. It is the first thing the fuzz step found, in the first run.

**Done for 135:** `services/graphql-bff/src/limits.ts` adds a validation rule: an operation is refused, before anything is resolved or called, when its nesting is deeper than 10, it has more than 8 fields directly under the operation (each is a call to a service) or more than 200 fields in all, counting fragments each time they are used (measured with a memo, so an exponential chain of fragments is measured in microseconds). The settings are `BFF_MAX_QUERY_DEPTH`, `BFF_MAX_ROOT_FIELDS` and `BFF_MAX_FIELDS`; a bad value stops the BFF naming the variable. The same rules apply to the event-stream path. The refusal is fixed text with the code `OPERATION_TOO_COMPLEX`. The queue board's query and the checkout mutation measure depth 6 or less and under 40 fields, so the limits are well above the apps. The 62 KB request with 1,328 aliases that made 57 downstream calls now makes none. **Decision left:** introspection stays on by default (the dynamic scan and tools use it); `BFF_DISABLE_INTROSPECTION=1` refuses it, and the lead decides whether a deployed environment sets it. Tests: `test/limits.test.ts` (14; the rule taken out fails 5 of them) and the alias test in the fuzz file now asserts no calls.

**137. The BFF's rate-limit backstop is counted per source address, which is the web app's server, so it is shared by every user of that web app**
Type: Design weakness · Source: the first trial of the load script, 2026-11-02 (`docs/evidence/WI-SEC-005/load-soak-2026-11-03.md`) · Hat: Security, Architect
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 (built, local only); the deployed dev environment still needs the setting (see below) · the web app sends the address it counted the browser by in `X-Bakery-Client-Address` (`packages/web-session`, both routes; never copied from the browser); the BFF believes it only from `BFF_TRUSTED_CLIENT_SOURCES` (`services/graphql-bff/src/rate-limit.ts`, `rateKey`); tests in `services/graphql-bff/test/client-address.test.ts` and the web-session proxy, handler and stream tests, each shown to fail with the trust check or the header removed; local scripts trust `127.0.0.1,::1` and Compose gives the web container the fixed address `172.28.0.10`; `WI-RATE-001` has a dated adjustment note. Run 2026-11-06: `node scripts/ci/skeleton-smoke.mjs --containers` passed (55 of 55) with the fixed web address and the trusted source in place; that run raises the limits, so it shows nothing broke, not that per-client counting works (the unit and integration tests show that). **Not done:** the dev Terraform does not set `BFF_TRUSTED_CLIENT_SOURCES` (the web container app's address in Azure is not fixed; with it unset the BFF keeps counting per connection, as before; to be settled with `WI-DEP-001`, item 119); ~~the load test has not been re-run~~ **Run 2026-11-07** (`docs/evidence/WI-SEC-005/client-address-load-2026-11-07.md`): with the backstop at 600 a minute, eight tight-loop readers sharing one counter left 51 of 54 orders refused; with each reader and the customer sending their own client address, 0 orders were refused outside the outage (55 placed). `load-test.mjs` gained `--bff-rate-limit` and `--client-addresses` for this (test tooling, for the lead's review; no product code changed). One run each, local only. The decision was: the lead chose option B: the web app passes the client's address in a header, the BFF trusts that header only from the web app's own address (a trusted-source setting, like `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS`), and counts per client. Callers that are not the trusted source (the Android app, direct calls) are still counted by their own address. Rate-limit trust logic is security-adjacent: the build needs the lead's approval of the design before code, tests that show a spoofed header from an untrusted source is ignored (and fail with the check removed), and an update of `WI-RATE-001`. To be scheduled as a work item.
`WI-RATE-001` gave the BFF a coarse ceiling of 600 requests a minute per source address. In practice the only source is the web app's server, so the ceiling is on the whole web app, not on a user. The web apps limit each address (600 a minute) and each login (120 a minute), so one client is held well below the BFF's ceiling; but a number of clients from different addresses, or one client with many logins, can together exceed 600 a minute and then every user of that web app is refused until the window passes: the backstop protects the BFF by turning overload into an outage for everyone, not by shedding the abusive caller. The load script's first trial showed it (two readers in a loop used the whole allowance and customers' orders were refused with a rate-limit error). "Reconciled" means choosing one of: raise the ceiling to a multiple of the expected peak and accept that it is a safety net only; make the web app pass the client's address to the BFF in a header the BFF trusts only from the web app's own address (a trusted-source setting, as `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` does for the web app) and count per client; or drop the BFF's counter and rely on the web app's per-login and per-address limits. The numbers in `WI-RATE-001` were the lead's starting values.


**138. Allergy-warning and low-stock notifications have no source event**
Type: Gap · Source: `NOT-001`/`NOT-002` scoping (2026-11-03) · Hat: Product
Status: Open
The allergy warning needs customer preference and allergy data (the personalization work), and the low-stock alert needs the catalog to publish a stock event; neither exists. `NOT-002` builds the order-lifecycle templates only. Add the two templates when their events exist. Also open: the contact lookup is seeded data; a real store and whether to batch reads are decided after measuring (`NOT-001`)
**Update 2026-11-04:** the allergy-warning half is **resolved by dropping it** (the lead, in the personalization scoping): the useful warning is before the order is placed (`WI-CUST-008`), and the useful message to the bakery is the allergens recorded on the order (`WI-ORD-009`, `WI-QUE-005`); `WI-PREF-002` is superseded. The low-stock alert still waits for a catalog stock event..

**139. The customer's order list reads the customer's whole partition for every page, and two-customer isolation was not tried on the real stack**
Type: Scaling limit and verification gap · Source: `WI-CUST-007` build (2026-11-04) · Hat: Architect, QA
Status: Open
`OrderStore.ListForCustomerAsync` reads every order of the customer and orders and pages them in memory, as the vendor list does (the reason is in `VendorOrderReader`: do not rely on the store query to order a composite cursor). A customer with a few hundred orders costs a few hundred small documents per page; revisit with a store-side ordering when that is no longer true. Also: the mock issuer has one customer, so "another customer's orders never appear" is proved by the ordering API tests (in-memory repository, partition and filter both mutation-checked), not on Cosmos with two customers. A second mock customer would close that.

**140. Real-stack journeys for `CUST-005` and `CUST-006` are written but have not run**
Type: Verification gap · Source: `CUST-005`/`CUST-006` build (2026-11-04) · Hat: QA
Status: Resolved 2026-11-04
`tests/skeleton/profile-page.spec.ts` (set an allergy in the browser, see the seeded croissant and sourdough marked, hide, clear, a vendor login refused) was not run: the `skeleton-smoke.mjs` run was stopped by the system for low memory (WSL), and the local stack was shut down at the lead's request. Both specs say so, and so do `WI-ORD-009` (`tests/skeleton/order-allergens.spec.ts`) `WI-QUE-005` (`tests/skeleton/queue-allergens.spec.ts`) `WI-CUST-008` (`tests/skeleton/checkout-allergens.spec.ts`) and `WI-TAG-002` and `WI-TAG-003` (`tests/skeleton/listing-tags.spec.ts`) and `WI-TAG-004` (`tests/skeleton/diet-preferences.spec.ts`). Next step: run `node scripts/ci/skeleton-smoke.mjs --containers` (`COMPOSE_PARALLEL_LIMIT=1`) when memory allows, fix what it finds, then remove the "Not run" notes.
Resolved: the full `skeleton-smoke.mjs --containers` run passed on a clean stack (55 of 55). It found, and the run fixed, mistakes in the specs themselves (the seed's option names; a restock that disturbed a journey's exact stock count), and one real gap: the web app's login rate limit (10 a minute, WI-RATE-001) stopped the browser journeys once there were enough of them, so the smoke run (and only it) now raises `RATE_LIMIT_LOGIN_PER_MINUTE` (`scripts/lib/local-stack.mjs`, `docker-compose.yml`); the demo and other environments keep the default. Not exercised by the run: the new fields on the real Cosmos container beyond what the journeys read back (they did round-trip through it), and ordering's Cosmos adapter tests (need the emulator).

**141. Decisions of 2026-11-04 (the lead)**
Type: Decision record · Source: session with the lead · Hat: Product
Status: Resolved 2026-11-04
ADR-0022 accepted; the nine allergens and labels approved; caps 12 per listing and 6 per option approved, all to be easy to adjust or extend (data, one setting). The cart acknowledgement **blocks with a confirm-and-override** (`CUST-008`). Browsing is unfiltered by default and allergen items stay selectable and customizable. Order allergens are carried by `OrderPlaced` and shown to the vendor through Ready until handover (`ORD-009`, `QUE-005`), so the bakery can label or stage the order; retention on the stream is left to the stream's own trimming (proposed, to confirm).

**142. Order allergens stay on the event stream as long as `OrderPlaced` does**
Type: Data retention · Source: `WI-ORD-009` build (2026-11-04) · Hat: Architect, Security
Status: Resolved 2026-11-04 (confirmed by the lead: no extra retention rule; the stream's own trimming applies)
`OrderPlaced` carries the allergens the customer chose for the order, and the Redis stream keeps its payloads (`Events:MaxStreamLength` 100000), so they live as long as the event. The only consumers are the vendor read model (this order's vendor) and the notification service (which does not read or print them). The data is order-scoped and customer-chosen, like the lines of the order, and is never the customer's profile. Proposed: no extra rule; the stream's own trimming applies. Confirm, or shorten the stream retention for this event, before anything is deployed. The same data also sits on the order and the vendor read model for as long as those are kept (`PRIV-001`, deferred).

**143. Public browse, with or without a tag filter, reads every published listing of every verified vendor and filters in memory**
Type: Scaling limit · Source: `WI-TAG-003` build (2026-11-04) · Hat: Architect
Status: Accepted 2026-11-04 (the lead agrees: fine at seed volume; revisit before real volume)
`ListingService.BrowseAsync` reads one partition per verified vendor and orders, filters and pages in memory (its own comment says so, and the personalized browse does the same). A tag filter adds one cheap pass over what was already read, so it does not change the cost class, but the cost grows with the number of listings and vendors, and a filter makes the read no cheaper. Fine at the seed volume (a handful of listings). Before real volume: a query by tag in Cosmos (an array-contains on the listing's tags) or a search index (`SRCH-*`), with the visibility and window rules kept in the catalog.

**144. A pipeline job stalled in the PR check of 2026-11-04 and was cancelled; the cause is unknown**
Type: Pipeline reliability · Source: PR 36 (`feature/slice-personalization-web`), reported by the lead · Hat: DevOps, QA
Status: Open
The lead reported that a check seemed hung and named the job "web-session package (real Redis)" (`azure-pipelines.yml`, job `session`: `docker compose up -d --wait redis-events`, install, format check, typecheck, `npm test`). The only log the lead could still see was the pipeline's initial YAML expansion, so the stalled sub-step is not known. **Not reproduced:** `npm test` in `packages/web-session` with `REDIS_CONNECTION_STRING=127.0.0.1:6379` and real Redis passed 172 of 172 in 9 s and exited by itself, and `scripts/ci/linux-check.mjs` (2 CPUs, 3 minute hang timeout) passed every step. Candidates: the Redis health wait or image pull on the agent, or an agent that stalled before the job. Next steps: when it happens again, note which sub-step shows the spinner and keep its last lines; consider a `timeoutInMinutes` on the jobs so a stall fails quickly instead of hanging. Separately, the same PR failed `order-allergens.spec.ts` once (the real-stack journey read the vendor order before the allergens were applied): fixed in the test (`WI-ORD-009`, a wait for the allergens), not a product defect.

**145. A native app's refresh token cannot be revoked at the issuer on sign-out or for a lost device**
Type: Known limitation · Source: ADR-0023 (consequences), `WI-MOB-001` · Hat: Security
Status: Accepted · trigger: a real identity provider replaces the mock issuer for the app
The Android app keeps a refresh token in the Keystore (`expo-secure-store`). The mock issuer rotates the token on every use and revokes the whole login if an already-used token is presented again (`RefreshTokenGrantTests`), but it has no way to revoke a login on request, so a token on a lost device works until it expires (8 hours by default, renewed on each use); signing out in the app only deletes the local copy. Local only; nothing is deployed. A real tenant (Entra) can revoke a session. "Reconciled" means revocation is tested against that provider before any real device is used.

**146. The mobile app has no allergen acknowledgement at the cart**
Type: Follow-up · Source: `WI-MOB-001` (open point), `WI-CUST-008` · Hat: Engineering, Product
Status: Resolved 2026-11-04
Resolution: built as `WI-MOB-002` (In review): the cart lists what each line contains, checkout warns and blocks Place order until the acknowledgement is ticked, says plainly when there is no profile or the check failed, and tells the bakery the customer's allergens; three Maestro flows pass on the emulator (`docs/evidence/WI-MOB-002/`). The allergen profile is persisted customer data read through the BFF, so no profile screen was part of it. What was not run is in that evidence file; the lead approved the wording on 2026-11-04.
The web app asks the customer to acknowledge allergen conflicts and can tell the bakery their allergens (`cartCheck`, the `allergens` field of `placeOrder`). The Android app does not: the BFF does not require it to place an order, which was the condition the spec set for building it, and the app shows no allergen or personalization screens. The shared package already holds the rules (`cartAllergens.ts`), so a follow-up item can add the screen. "Reconciled" means a work item exists or the lead decides the app stays without it.

**147. The debug build of the app could not load its JavaScript from the Metro dev server on the emulator; the bundle is embedded instead**
Type: Tooling limitation · Source: `WI-MOB-001` · Hat: Engineering
Status: Accepted · trigger: someone needs hot reload on the emulator
On the first debug build the app showed "Unable to load script" although Metro answered on the host, `adb reverse tcp:8081 tcp:8081` was set and the bundle compiled; the cause was not found. `apps/mobile-customer/plugins/withEmbeddedBundle.js` makes the debug build carry its own bundle (`debuggableVariants = []`), which the Maestro flows need anyway (no dev server). The build type stays debug, so the cleartext http manifest stays debug-only (ADR-0023). Cost: a JavaScript change needs a Gradle build (25 to 80 s). Also found: adding `react-native-safe-area-context` breaks the CMake step on Windows (path too long), so the header uses `StatusBar.currentHeight` instead.

**148. The mobile flows run only on the lead's machine and depend on the emulator image's state**
Type: Test limitation · Source: `WI-MOB-001` · Hat: QA
Status: Accepted
Decided by the lead: Android only, local only, no CI, no Maestro cloud. The flows (`apps/mobile-customer/.maestro/`, run by `node scripts/dev/mobile-flows.mjs`) log in through Chrome Custom Tabs; Chrome's first-run screen ("Use without an account") appears once on a fresh emulator image and the login subflow dismisses it only if it shows. A different image or Chrome version may show other prompts. The app under test is a test build (decline switch on) and the BFF runs with `ENABLE_TEST_HOOKS=1`: a test setup, never a demo (`scripts/dev/mobile-stack.mjs`). The app has no accessibility or visual checks yet. The refused-session, expired-token and failed-request paths are not covered by a flow or a unit test: `WI-MOB-003`. **Added 2026-11-04 (`WI-MOB-002`):** (1) Chrome sometimes shows the issuer's login page with its heading but not its buttons in the accessibility tree (seen once in five runs, "Element not found" after 20 s); the login subflow falls back to tapping where that user's button is (a position computed from its row in the issuer's list), and whether that fallback ever ran, or is right for each of the five customers, was not checked. (2) Flows 05 to 10 log in as seeded customers (their saved allergen profiles are seed data, `docs/operations/local-dev.md`); flows 05, 06 and 08 to 10 read a customer's newest order and flow 07 stops and starts the BFF through a local control server that only `scripts/dev/mobile-flows.mjs` starts (`scripts/lib/flow-control.mjs`, `127.0.0.1:4199`, which signs in as the customer asked for with the app's own flow); started by `maestro test` alone they fail at that step (register 150). It has no password: it refuses requests from a browser page (an `Origin` header), without its custom header or with the wrong `Host` (8 tests), but another program running as the same user can call it during a run. (3) A run can be killed by low memory: the emulator, the Docker stack and a Gradle build together are heavy on this machine (once, no result). (4) The laptop going into Windows Modern Standby during a run stalled one flow for 29 minutes (the power log matches); keep the machine awake. (5) Every flow of a run shares one stack, so stock is a budget (the Almond Croissant has 4 units and only flows 05 and 10 may buy it); a flow that sells out an item another needs fails with it shown as "Sold out" (written in the app's README). (6) A YAML mistake in a flow stops Maestro at parsing after everything is up; the runner now checks the syntax first.

**149. The customer web app shows an empty coloured box under the "hide items" box on the browse page**
Type: Defect (cosmetic) · Source: the demo screenshot `docs/operations/demo/07-browse-conflicts.png` (2026-11-04), `apps/web-customer/src/components/ListingsList.tsx` · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-04
When a signed-in customer has a profile, the browse page renders `<p role="status" data-testid="hidden-count">` with an empty string until the customer ticks a hiding box, and the shared `[role='status']` style (padding, rounded background) draws it as an empty green bar. Found while taking screenshots for `demo.md`; the cause is read from the source and the screenshot, not reproduced in a browser test. Not fixed here (it is in the personalization web work, outside `WI-MOB-002`). "Reconciled" means the element renders nothing when it has no text, or the style does not apply to an empty one, and a browser test asserts it.
Resolution: an empty `alert`, `note` or `status` box now takes no room and shows no colour (`globals.css`: `:empty` sets no padding, margin or background; it is not `display: none`, so a live region stays in the page for screen readers). A browser test (`personalized.spec.ts`, "the “hidden” message takes no room ...") asserts the empty message is under 1 px high with no background, and a real one is a message again; it failed (22 px) before the fix and passes after it. The whole customer browser suite passes (133 tests, accessibility scans included). The nine demo screenshots were retaken against the real stack after the fix, and the browse one (`07-browse-conflicts.png`) was looked at: the empty bar is gone.

**150. Review the test-only mechanisms built for the Android flows (the control server, the app's decline switch)**
Type: Design review · Source: the lead, 2026-11-04 (`WI-MOB-002`); the collaboration note in `CLAUDE.md` ("Don't build functionality only to close a gap in testing") · Hat: Architect, QA
Status: Open · part 2 built 2026-11-06 as `WI-ORD-010` (the decline switch, `EXPO_PUBLIC_TEST_HOOKS`, the BFF's `X-Test-Payment` forwarding and `ENABLE_TEST_HOOKS` setting, the stub's decline branch and the proxy's header allowance are removed; a declined payment is a seeded method with an invalid token); part 1 (the flow-control server) stays open as test tooling. Run 2026-11-07: the host real-stack smoke (56 of 56) and the ordering tests with Redis and Cosmos up (522 passed, 0 skipped). Also run 2026-11-07: the container smoke (56 of 56) and the Maestro flows (a cold run passed 12 of 14; flow 07 and the new no-payment-methods flow were fixed and passed in their own runs, the latter renumbered 13 so the issuer-restart flow stays last as 14). Not yet run: all fourteen in one cold run, and `load-test.mjs` for item 137. The web app's `x-fixture` and `x-delay-ms` headers behind `ENABLE_TEST_HOOKS` remain (item 155) · (earlier: spec drafted as `WI-ORD-010`; (1) the flow-control server is kept as test tooling (it drives the system from outside; no product code uses it); (2) the decline switch and the BFF's `X-Test-Payment` forwarding are to be replaced by data. Finding when this was decided: the checkout request carries no payment instrument at all (`CheckoutRequest` has vendor, lines, pickup window, allergens), so there is no card token to make invalid; the stub is told to decline only by the test header. Decided 2026-11-06 (the lead chose option A): a simple payment-method token. Each customer has saved payment methods (an opaque token and a description), checkout shows them in a dropdown, the stub rejects an invalid token as a declined payment, and the seed data holds at least one invalid token (a named, self-describing fixture) so a flow reaches the decline by choosing it, with no header and no switch. To do: a work item (spec first, for the lead's approval: it changes the checkout contract, the BFF schema and both customer apps, and sits next to the payment design of ADR-0007, so the token must stay opaque and no card data is stored); when it is built, remove the app's decline switch (`EXPO_PUBLIC_TEST_HOOKS`) and the BFF's `X-Test-Payment` forwarding, and the ordering hook, with D-10 amended. Fault injection by stopping the BFF stays acceptable tooling. See item 155 for the wider review of mocking and test scope.)
Two things were built only to let tests set something up or reach something, and the lead asked for them to be reviewed:
1. **The flow-control server** (`scripts/lib/flow-control.mjs`, started by `scripts/dev/mobile-flows.mjs`, `127.0.0.1:4199`). It was built to do three things a Maestro flow cannot: set Alice's allergen profile (a gap in the seed data, which had one customer; **removed 2026-11-04**, see below), read a customer's newest order to check what the bakery was told, and stop the BFF and start it again (for the "allergy check failed" flow).
