The last two remain. **Added 2026-11-05 (`WI-MOB-003`, named in the spec): `GET /order-count` (how many orders a customer has, to show a retry placed exactly one) and `POST /issuer/restart` (restarts the mock issuer's container so it forgets its refresh tokens, for the "refused session" flow; the issuer signs with a new key afterwards, so that flow must be the last of a run). Both are behind the same guard and have tests; no new endpoint on the issuer or any product service.** It signs in with the app's own PKCE flow, refuses browser calls, has no password (register 148, 2). Product code uses none of it.
2. **The app's "decline the payment" switch** (`EXPO_PUBLIC_TEST_HOOKS=1`, `CheckoutScreen`) and the BFF's `X-Test-Payment: decline` forwarding (`ENABLE_TEST_HOOKS=1`, first-slice contract D-10, an earlier decision). The switch exists only so a flow can reach the declined-payment path; it is compiled out of any other build.
Questions for the review: can a failed order be reached with data alone (a seeded listing or customer whose order the payment stub declines)? Should the "told to the bakery" check be a screen (the order page shows the allergens, which the web lacks too) instead of a read through a side server? Is a fault-injection step (stopping the BFF) acceptable tooling, or should the failure be reached another way (a service answering an error for a seeded case)? "Reconciled" means each mechanism is kept with a stated reason, replaced by data or an existing interface, or removed, and this item records which. Progress 2026-11-04: **the profile endpoint was replaced by seeded customers** (four mock customers with saved profiles written by the seed tool, `docs/operations/local-dev.md`; the lead ruled that this is seed data, not auth logic), and the flows that needed run-time profiles now log in as those customers; the other two mechanisms are still open for the review.

**151. The Android app deletes the stored refresh token when the issuer answers 5xx**
Type: Defect (auth logic) · Source: `WI-MOB-003` unit tests (2026-11-05), `apps/mobile-customer/src/auth/session.ts` `postToken` · Hat: Engineering, Security
Status: Resolved 2026-11-05 · the lead approved the change; `postToken` now answers unreachable for a status of 500 or more (one line, `session.ts`); the test is a normal test and a mutation (the line removed) makes it fail. A 429 is still treated as a refusal (the issuer's rate limit; not changed, not tested).
`postToken` returns 'refused' for every answer that is not 2xx. A 500, 502 or 503 from the issuer (a deploy, an overload) therefore ends the session and deletes the refresh token from the Keystore, and the customer must log in again. The spec of `WI-MOB-003` and the web session (`packages/web-session`, where a 5xx is an outage) both keep the token; ADR-0023 decision 5 says the token is deleted only when the issuer refuses it. The unit test "an issuer 5xx is an outage, not a refusal" states the required behaviour; it is marked todo so the suite stays green, and it fails today (the token is gone). The change would be one line: answer 'unreachable' for a status of 500 or more. Not made, because it is session code and the item says changes there go to the lead first.

**152. After the retry, a second UNAUTHENTICATED answer leaves the app signed in**
Type: Question (behaviour) · Source: `WI-MOB-003` unit tests (2026-11-05), `apps/mobile-customer/src/api.ts` · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-06 (built; test in `apps/mobile-customer/tests/api.test.ts` fails without it; note on ADR-0023; an unreachable issuer keeps the session; Maestro flows not re-run) · the lead chose B: when the retry after a renewal is rejected too, the session ends (state signedOut, refresh token deleted, login screen). Reason: a user stuck on error screens is worse than a login prompt, and a fresh sign-in gets a fresh token; keeping the token would only sign the user in silently into the same failure on the next start. To build: the change in `api.ts` (session code, approved by this choice), the unit tests changed to assert it (and shown to fail without the change), the `WI-MOB-003` spec wording kept, and a dated note at the bottom of ADR-0023 (the token is deleted when the issuer refuses it, or when the BFF rejects a token the issuer has just issued). A device clock more than the services' 2-minute tolerance (`AuthServiceExtensions.cs`) off the server is one way to reach the case.
When the BFF answers UNAUTHENTICATED, the app renews the access token once and retries once (tested: no loop). If the retry is rejected as well, `gql` throws an ApiError with the code UNAUTHENTICATED but the session state stays signedIn (the issuer accepted the refresh, so nothing ends the session). Screens that check for that code show their own message; the login screen does not appear. The spec wording ("ends in the sign-in state") suggests it should. It only happens when the issuer and the BFF disagree about a token (a misconfigured audience, a clock difference), so it is a robustness question, not a known path. The tests assert the current behaviour (an error, one renewal, one retry).

**153. First SonarQube Community scan: 15 bugs, 37 vulnerabilities, 224 code smells to triage**
Type: Finding (static analysis) · Source: `node scripts/ci/sonar-scan.mjs` on commit 5bb2cbd (2026-11-05) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Tracking · triaged 2026-11-06 (`docs/evidence/sonar/triage-2026-11-06.md`): no real application defect among the 15 bugs; the cheap code smells fixed; Terraform findings moved to item 156; 2026-11-06 later: `CA1848` fixed (53 logging calls now use source-generated `[LoggerMessage]` methods; the one generic helper `LogStructuredEvent` is suppressed with a reason; the old `.editorconfig` suppression was removed) and the constant arrays hoisted in `MockOidcIssuer`, `ServiceAuth` and `SeedData`; the 34 `CA1861` hits left were in tests and are now switched off for tests in `.editorconfig` (product code still warns); `CA1873` and `CA1859` set to suggestion; the auth and checkout findings fixed in one commit for the lead to review (`CA1510`, `CA1850`, `CA1852`, `CS8601`, `ASP0025`, `CS1717`/`CS8602`); still open: `S8949` in `InMemoryBroadcast.Start`. The two `web-session` hits are resolved 2026-11-05
First run of the local SonarQube scan (C# and TypeScript, 21,345 lines of code, no tests run so no coverage). The quality gate passed, but it only judges new code; the overall ratings are reliability D, security E, maintainability A, duplication 2.2%. Nothing was fixed in this change. What the 37 "vulnerabilities" are: 19 are S4036 (a command found through the PATH) in our own scripts (`scripts/`), 4 are S2245 (`Math.random`), the rest are Terraform rules (`infra/environments/dev` 7, `infra/foundation` 2) and one Dockerfile rule. Two of the S2245 hits are in `packages/web-session/src/store.ts` (lines 111 and 185): the owner token of the refresh lock is built from `Math.random()`. It is not a session id or a credential, so the risk looks low, but it is session code, the lead decided on 2026-11-05 to use `crypto.randomUUID()`, and both lock tokens now do (the 172 unit tests pass; the 13 Redis store tests were skipped, Redis was not running, so the Redis lock path is not re-run). The 15 bugs are mostly S2871 (array sort without a comparer) and a few C# rules. Next step: triage in the kept server (`--keep`), mark the false positives, fix the rest in a work item. Adding the scan to the pipeline is item 154.

**154. Option: run the SonarQube scan in the pipeline**
Type: Option (future) · Source: lead, 2026-11-05 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Open · not scheduled
The scan runs only locally (`node scripts/ci/sonar-scan.mjs`, item 153). If it should run on every build, there are two ways: a SonarQube server container started inside the pipeline job (no account, no cost, but about 8 minutes and a large image on every run, and nothing remembered between runs, so it cannot tell new code from old), or SonarQube Cloud with its Azure DevOps extension (remembers history and judges new code, but needs an account and a token in the pipeline variables, and the free plan's limits for a private repository must be checked first). Either way it would start as a soft, non-blocking job like the other first runs. Decide after the findings of item 153 are triaged, so the first pipeline run does not start with a long list of old findings.

**155. Agenda: review what is mocked, what is intercepted, and what each kind of test covers; consider Selenium**
Type: Process review (agenda) · Source: lead, 2026-11-06 (while deciding item 150) · Hat: Quality, Architect
Status: Open · to be taken up after the current register walk-through
Scope the lead named: (1) what each suite mocks or intercepts (the mock BFF and stubbed issuer of the browser suites, the fake issuer, Playwright route interception, in-memory backends, the declined-payment header, the flow-control server) and what each therefore cannot show; (2) the scope of each kind of test (unit, contract, adapter against real Docker, mock-backed browser, real-stack smoke, Maestro) written down in one place and compared with `test-strategy.md`; (3) whether to add Selenium (for example for cross-browser runs against the real stack, item 132) alongside Playwright, and what it would add. "Reconciled" means a short review document with a table of test layers, what is real and what is faked in each, the gaps, and the lead's decision on Selenium. Related: items 132, 148, 150. Added 2026-11-06: `forwardTestHeaders` in `apps/web-customer/src/lib/relay/environment.ts` (the `x-fixture` and `x-delay-ms` headers behind `ENABLE_TEST_HOOKS=1`, used by the mock-backed browser suite) is a test-only mechanism in product code; it is outside `WI-ORD-010` (which removes only the payment part of the flag) and belongs in this review.

**156. The Sonar scan flags nine Terraform design points for the deployed environment**
Type: Deployment follow-up · Source: the triage of item 153, 2026-11-06 · Hat: Platform Engineering, Security
Status: Open · for the deployment-prep work (`WI-DEP-001`, ADR-0018); nothing changed
In `infra/foundation/main.tf` and `infra/environments/dev/main.tf`: public network access is not disabled on the foundation and dev resources (S6329, rated BLOCKER, two hits; at `foundation:21` and `dev:166`); no managed identity block (S6378, two); purge protection off on the Key Vault (S8847; deliberate, so the dev environment can be torn down); no `client_certificate_mode` on three container apps (S6382); no `backup.retention_in_hours` (S6364). "Reconciled" means each is set deliberately or accepted with a reason written next to the resource, before the first real apply, and the Terraform tests cover the ones set.

**157. Payments beyond the stub: add-payment-method and capture are not scoped**
Type: Scope gap · Source: the lead, 2026-11-06 (while deciding `WI-ORD-010`) · Hat: Product, Architect
Status: Open · not in demo scope · part (2) decided 2026-11-06: capture at confirm, as ADR-0005 says (no amendment needed); part (1) stays open
(1) Customers cannot add, edit or remove a payment method; the methods are seeded (`WI-ORD-010`). Adding one has not been scoped or designed (ADR-0005's hosted fields would return the token). (2) `WI-ORD-010` makes the order keep the payment method id and the authorization reference, so a later item can charge. When to charge is not settled: ADR-0005 and the `IPaymentProcessor` comment say capture at confirm; the lead said charge when fulfilled or completed. "Reconciled" means a decision on the capture event (with an ADR-0005 amendment if it differs), and either a spec for add-payment-method or a recorded decision that it stays out of scope.

**158. Listing cache: points decided or left open when `WI-CAT-003`, `WI-PORT-004` and `WI-CUST-009` were rewritten**
Type: Decision · Source: the lead and this session, 2026-11-07 · Hat: Architect, Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead closed it, which covers the review of the stock-path diff in points 4 and 7; register 159 was already resolved; the cache Redis for the dev environment stays with `WI-DEP-001`, nothing deployed) · `WI-CAT-003` built 2026-11-07 (In review, `feature/listing-cache`); `WI-CUST-009` built 2026-11-07 (In review; the Maestro flows not re-run with it); `WI-PORT-004` built 2026-11-07 (In review); the items below are for the lead or the build session
(1) **Decided 2026-11-07:** no "hide out of stock" option; sold-out listings stay in browse with their status. Stock is checked at add to the order (fresh read, `WI-CUST-009`) and at checkout (atomic reservation, ADR-0017). Invalidation happens when a listing's **status** changes, not on every sale. (2) **Decided 2026-11-07:** when the fresh stock read at add fails, the item is not added and the customer is told (`WI-CUST-009`). (3) **Decided 2026-11-07:** one cache key per vendor holding the full list, not one per listing; revisit if a vendor's list grows large or single-listing reads dominate. (4) **Open, review:** the cached listing document holds the owner-only quantity and threshold in server memory or Redis; the spec requires the DTO to strip them and a test, and the lead reviews the stock-code diff. (5) **Known limit:** with several replicas on the local fallback an invalidation reaches only its own replica; the 60 second TTL bounds it (ADR-0002). (6) **Measured 2026-11-07** (`docs/evidence/WI-CAT-003/after-2026-11-07.md`, three runs each, the in-process provider, the seeded catalog): browse p50 about 20 ms to about 3 ms, home page about 24 ms to about 8 ms, checkout unchanged; the Redis provider's speed and a larger catalog were not measured. (7) **Open, for the lead's review:** the stock-path invalidation in `StockReservationService` and `ListingService.AdjustStockAsync` (item 4); stampede protection (single flight) was added on 2026-11-07 and is per process; the dev Terraform and Compose do not give the catalog a cache Redis (it defaults to the local provider). "Reconciled" means the lead has reviewed the stock-path diff (4), the cache Redis is decided for the dev environment (`WI-DEP-001`), and register 159 is decided.

**159. The session proxy's own refusals and 502 responses carry no Cache-Control**
Type: Hardening gap · Source: `WI-PORT-004`, 2026-11-07 (the layer 3 header test) · Hat: Security, Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-07 · the lead chose option 2 with `no-store`: a default rule for `/api/:path*` in both apps' `next.config.js` (no change to the shared `packages/web-session`); tests `apps/web-customer/tests/e2e/cache-headers.spec.ts` and `apps/web-queue-display/tests/e2e/cache-headers.spec.ts` (an origin refusal, a 413 and an unknown route; removing the rule makes the first two fail, shown); both browser suites pass (144 and 61). Not asserted through the app: that a stream keeps its own `no-store, no-transform` (checked in the session package's unit tests), and a 502 for an unreachable BFF (no test can take the mock BFF away inside the suite)
`proxyGraphql` and `proxyGraphqlStream` copy the BFF's headers (the BFF sends `private, no-store`) and, on a success, set `no-store, no-transform` themselves on the stream path; but the responses the proxy makes itself carry no `Cache-Control`: the origin refusal, a 413, a 502 for an unreachable or unconfigured BFF, and the non-stream path trusts the upstream header. ADR-0014 says each response type sets an explicit policy. The risk is low (they are answers to POST requests, a browser does not reuse those without explicit freshness, and they hold no data), so this is a tidiness and defence-in-depth item. "Reconciled" means the lead decides: set `Cache-Control: no-store` on every response the proxy itself writes (with a test per refusal), or accept the gap with a reason.

**160. `WI-ORD-003`: the strategy is agreed; built 2026-11-07, in review**
Type: Planned work · Source: the lead's reconciliation of the demo and the fulfillment seam, 2026-11-07 (`docs/product/backlog/reconciliation/RECONCILIATION-DISPOSITIONS-2026-11-07.md`) · Hat: Architect, Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead reviewed and closed it) · built 2026-11-07 (In review) · decision 2 made 2026-11-07 (a consumer name on the subscriber interface); the build, its tests and what was not run are in `WI-ORD-003.md` "As built"; "reconciled" now means the lead reviews it
Agreed: `PickupFulfillment` as a separate consumer; "Handed over" answered with 202 and completed through `OrderFulfillmentConfirmationRequested` and `OrderFulfillmentCompleted` (a plain event published by the endpoint); a "still waiting, try again" note after about 15 s on the vendor screen; the customer's "ready" message from `OrderReadyForPickup` (and a pickup-window message) with additive payload fields. **Open:** how a second consumer inside ordering-service gets its own consumer group, because the transport's group is per service and event type and two subscribers would split the messages: a second transport identity (nothing shared changes; only Redis tests can catch a collision; dead letters do not name the consumer) or a `consumerName` on `IEventSubscriber.SubscribeAsync` (a change to the shared library, both transports and the contract tests; a duplicate group in one process can fail at start-up; recommended). "Reconciled" means decision 2 is made and `WI-ORD-003` is built, tested (including the test that Ordering's core has no reference to the strategy's types) and reviewed, and ADR-0001, `order-lifecycle.md` and `WI-ORD-007` say what was built.

**161. Android checkout: the saved payment methods load after the screen opens and move the place button**
Type: Usability · Source: the first run of Maestro flow `13-cancel-order`, 2026-11-07 · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-07 · the loading line and the list now share a fixed-height area (two rows, `PAYMENT_AREA_HEIGHT = 100`; more than two methods scroll inside it), so the list arriving no longer moves Place order; the failure and "none saved" notices sit outside the area (with no method chosen the button is off and a tap does nothing). Run on the emulator: flows `11-failed-checkout-request` (55 s), `13-cancel-order` (52 s) and `13-no-payment-methods` (34 s) pass, each run alone. **Not shown:** that the button does not move (Maestro cannot assert a position; no layout check was written), and the other twelve flows after the change (a full run was stopped by memory pressure). A first version (an area sized for three rows) was run and made flow 11 fail, because the extra height pushed Place order below the fold when the rejection notice was shown; the two-row version replaced it. One unrelated failure of flow 11 in the full run, `POST /bff/stop`: the BFF did not go down within 60 s, did not repeat (item 164).
Found by a test. `CheckoutScreen` loaded the customer's payment methods after it opens; the list replaces a "Loading payment methods…" line and pushes the place button down. The place button is off until a method is chosen, but a tap on it at the instant the list arrives can land on a payment button underneath and choose it (the run chose "Test card (refused)" and the order was then refused). The flow now waits for the list and chooses the accepted card. For a person it is a small chance; "reconciled" means the list reserves its space (a fixed-height placeholder) or the button stays where it is, with a Maestro or unit check.

**162. Dependency audit: moderate advisories in dev tooling and Expo build tooling, deprecated test packages, and the pipeline question**
Type: Decision and hardening · Source: `WI-SEC-004`, the first dependency audit, 2026-11-07 (`docs/evidence/WI-SEC-004/dependency-audit-2026-11-07.md`) · Hat: Platform Engineering, Security
Status: Open · nothing fixed, decisions are the lead's
No critical or high advisory, no vulnerable NuGet package. Open: (1) 25 moderate npm advisories from two root causes, `minimatch` (GHSA-g3hv-75wg-m249) and `glob` (GHSA-3g3m-2fjr-6pw4): in ESLint and typescript-eslint of the web apps and the BFF (development only; production dependencies of those three are clean) and in Expo's tooling of the Android app, which npm counts as production (the audit cannot say whether the vulnerable code runs inside the installed app). The fixes npm offers are breaking: ESLint 9.39, `eslint-config-next` 15.5, `typescript-eslint` 8.36 and `expo@57`. Option: upgrade the lint chain in one small change per project with the gates as the check, and Expo with the next planned Expo upgrade; or accept as development tooling with this record as the reason. (2) `xunit` 2.5.3 is marked legacy in the three service test projects (alternative `xunit.v3`); a migration, not a vulnerability. (3) Whether to add the audit as a pipeline job (it needs `npm ci` in six projects and the network, a few minutes on the agent) and whether the proposed fail policy (high or critical in production npm, any vulnerable NuGet package) is the one to enforce; `azure-pipelines.yml` is a shared file. (4) A container image scan (the Compose and Dockerfile images) was not run. "Reconciled" means the lead decides each of the four.

**162 (addendum 2026-11-11).** The repository was made public and Dependabot raised five alerts, all the two advisories above: #1 `glob` (GHSA-3g3m-2fjr-6pw4) and #2 `minimatch` (GHSA-g3hv-75wg-m249) in `apps/mobile-customer`, and #3, #4 and #5 `minimatch`, development only, in `graphql-bff`, `web-queue-display` and `web-customer`. `npm audit` on the lockfiles the same day shows a **non-breaking** fix for the Android app (`glob` and `minimatch` within the ranges Expo allows; on 2026-11-07 it was breaking), while for the BFF and the web apps the fix npm offers is a **downgrade** of ESLint to 9.39.5 and typescript-eslint to 8.36.0, which is not recommended. Exploitability is low: both are denial-of-service issues that need an attacker-supplied glob pattern or cancellation signal, the patterns here come from our own configuration, and the web apps and the BFF have no advisory in their production dependencies. **Proposed, not decided (waiting for the lead's go-ahead):** a separate branch `feature/dependency-fixes` and pull request; in `apps/mobile-customer` run `npm audit fix`, check the diff is lockfile only, then format, typecheck and unit tests, and one run of the Android flows for the build tooling; in the BFF and the two web apps try an `overrides` pin of `minimatch` to 11.0.1 or later and run lint, typecheck and tests, and if lint breaks (ESLint is built against minimatch 10) dismiss the three alerts in Dependabot as development-only tooling and revisit when ESLint updates; leave Dependabot security updates and version updates off so changes stay one branch at a time; note the outcome here.

**162 (addendum 2026-11-12, the five alerts fixed locally, not pushed).** The lead said go, on the current branch (no separate branch). Done: (a) `apps/mobile-customer`: `npm audit fix` (run twice) moved `glob` within Expo's range and many patch and minor versions of Babel and Expo packages (the lockfile only), but `@expo/fingerprint@0.21.10` still asked for `minimatch` ^10.2.6 and npm's only other fix was `expo@52` (a downgrade), so `package.json` got a scoped `overrides` entry (`@expo/fingerprint` to `minimatch` ^11.0.1, installed 11.0.8). (b) The BFF and the two web apps: `overrides` of `minimatch` ^11.0.1 scoped to `eslint` and `typescript-eslint` (the two that bring in 10.x; not global, because `eslint-plugin-import`, `-react` and `-jsx-a11y` in the web apps use `minimatch` 3.1.5, outside the advisory range, and 3 to 11 changes the export shape). `npm audit` reports 0 vulnerabilities in all four projects. Checks run: format, lint, typecheck and unit tests in the BFF (pass), format, lint and typecheck in both web apps (pass; the customer app has no unit tests), queue display 40 of 40 unit tests, Android app format, typecheck and 45 of 45 unit tests. **Run later the same day:** all 15 Android Maestro flows on the emulator against the real stack, in one cold run: 15 of 15 passed in 9m 15s. **Not run:** the web apps' Playwright suites; the pipeline. The overrides are a workaround: remove them when ESLint, typescript-eslint and `@expo/fingerprint` themselves require `minimatch` 11.0.1 or later. Points (2) to (4) of the record are unchanged.

**163. Demo film: scripted, never run; sync accuracy and the first real runs are open**
Type: Planned work · Source: the lead's plan for a three-screen demo video (web customer, phone customer, vendor queue, several customers in parallel), 2026-11-07 · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-16 (closed by the lead) · was: Open · scripted 2026-11-07, not run. The plan, the storyboard, how it works, what was and was not checked and the order of the first runs are in `docs/operations/demo-film.md`; the scripts are `scripts/dev/film/film.mjs` (director), `compose.mjs` (video) and `record-phone.mjs` (phone), and the phone's story is `apps/mobile-customer/.maestro/demo/phone-max.yaml`. Decisions made with the lead: no fixed length (about two minutes, cap two and a half); the phone recording starts when the app is on its first screen; scenarios are allergens on web and phone, several other customers' orders with different combinations (plain, option, pickup time, allergy told to the bakery), the vendor's steps and a cancel, with several seconds between beats; captions in line with the action and no mention of how the orders are placed; the video stays local for the lead's review (a GitHub release is the lead's to decide).
What the spike showed (`scripts/dev/film/record-phone.mjs`, `ffmpeg` 9.0.2 installed with winget, a three-source composite made from the phone recording and two stand-ins): (1) the emulator records with `adb shell screenrecord` at 540x1200, about 35 fps while the screen changes and almost nothing while it is still (frames are written only on change, so a still period makes the file's timeline shorter than the wall clock: 117 s of video for 181 s of wall time in one chunk); the film must re-time each chunk to the wall clock (start times are in `phone.json`) before composing. (2) `screenrecord` stops after 3 minutes, so the recorder writes chunks; about a second can be lost between two. (3) The last chunk is pulled only after it ends: if the emulator is torn down first, that footage is lost (it happened when `mobile-flows.mjs` tore the emulator down before the recorder was stopped); the film script must stop the recorder, wait for the pull, then stop the emulator. (4) Early chunks failed while Android was still starting; start the recorder after the emulator is up. (5) A composite of three sources with captions renders in seconds at 1920x1080 (layout in `artifacts/film/spike/compose.sh`, not committed: artifacts are git-ignored). Test tooling that drives the system only from outside (the recorder watches; the later film script will drive the apps and place background orders through the API, and says so). Open: the film script (three actors, a vendor agent that acts on whatever is in the queue, background customers), wall-clock sync accuracy (not measured), the storyboard, where the video lives (kept local for the lead's review; a GitHub release is the lead's to decide). "Reconciled" means the film exists, was reviewed by the lead, and the sync was checked.
Resolution: the film was run and composed on 2026-11-08 (the run without the phone twice, the full run with the phone four times, each composed and checked; the phone chunks re-timed to the wall clock by `compose.mjs`) and committed as `docs/operations/demo/demo-film.mp4`, linked from the README. What the runs found and fixed is in `docs/operations/demo-film.md`, "Where it ended up". Playing it inline on GitHub's front page is item 190.

**164. Android flow 11: one unexplained BFF-stop timeout in a full run**
Type: Flaky test tooling · Source: the full Maestro run of 2026-11-07 · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-09 · cause found and fixed: flow `07` starts the BFF twice (in the flow and in its `onFlowComplete`); the second start died on the taken port and, as the last `bff` entry, hid the live process from `stopOne`, so flow 11's stop did nothing. `startOne` in `scripts/lib/local-stack.mjs` now does nothing when a live copy runs. A full run failed on exactly this (14 passed, flow 11 failed), and after the fix all 15 flows passed in one run (9 min 45 s).
Original report: in the first full run after the register 161 change, flow `11-failed-checkout-request` failed at `POST /bff/stop` of the flow-control server: `http://localhost:4100/healthz did not go down within 60 s` (the BFF process was asked to stop and kept answering). The same flow passed when run alone the next time (55 s). Not diagnosed: it may be a slow process-tree kill on Windows under memory pressure or a stream the BFF held open; the flow-control server's stop (`scripts/lib/flow-control.mjs`, `stack.stopOne('bff')`) is the place to look if it repeats.

**165. A catalog failure during checkout reaches the customer as a generic error**
Type: Gap · Source: the caching, fallback and degradation review (register item 89), 2026-11-08 · Hat: Architect
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (the lead chose the code and the wording, and a seeded scenario for the journey). Ordering is unchanged (it already answered `503 downstream_unavailable` for the catalog, the payment processor and its store, register 170). The BFF lets a 503 through only with that code and maps it to a new `PlaceOrderErrorCode`, `TEMPORARILY_UNAVAILABLE`, with the fixed text "We could not place your order right now. Nothing has been charged, and your order is still here. Please try again in a moment."; any other 503 or 5xx is still `INTERNAL`. It is the one rejection that does not spend its idempotency key: `rejectionSpendsKey` in `packages/customer-core`, used by the web and Android checkouts, which both keep the cart. Journey: the new `catalog-outage` scenario (ordering's catalog layer fails every call) and its spec, run on the real stack by `node scripts/ci/chaos-smoke.mjs catalog-outage`: 1 of 1 passed (two presses, both typed and without a reference, the same key, no order, stock unchanged; `docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-003/summary-catalog-outage.json`). Also run: the BFF's place-order tests (35 of 35), `customer-core` (117 of 117), the Android app's unit tests (45 of 45), typecheck and lint. Not run: the Android flows (the screen change is the same one-line rule) and a run with the catalog process itself stopped (the chaos layer throws the exception the real gateway throws for a refused connection, so only the 5 s timeout is not exercised).
When the catalog is down or slow, ordering cannot price or reserve and answers `503 downstream_unavailable` (`CheckoutService.cs`); its call to the catalog times out at 5 s. The BFF has no typed code for it (`resolvers.ts` maps the other ordering codes, not this one), so the customer sees "Something went wrong" with a trace reference, and the web and Android clients cannot offer "try again in a moment". Read in code; the BFF's timeout is tested against a stub that never answers, but nothing runs checkout against a stopped catalog. Reconciled means: a typed `PlaceOrderRejected` code (or a retryable flag) mapped in the BFF, shown by both clients, and a real-stack journey that stops the catalog during a checkout. A change to the GraphQL schema and to what a customer is told, so the lead decides the wording and whether the cart is kept.

**166. Failure of the issuer, Cosmos and the cache Redis has not been exercised against the real dependency**
Type: Verification gap · Source: the caching, fallback and degradation review (register item 89), 2026-11-08 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-18 (run at the lead's go-ahead on a quiet machine)
Resolution 2026-11-18: each of the three was stopped on the local stack while customers were using it (browser steps as a customer, a token probe, and a steady load of reads and orders) and started again; recorded in `docs/evidence/dependency-outages-2026-11-18.md`, and the rows of `docs/architecture/patterns.md` section 14 say what was seen. All three recovered by themselves, with no service restarted and no data lost. Issuer down: signed-in customers unaffected, a new sign-in ends on the browser's connection error. Cache Redis down: no read failed, the catalog went back to Redis by itself. Cosmos down: browsing continued while cached, the history and checkout pages said they could not load. What the runs found is in three new items: 204 (after the mock issuer restarts, new sign-ins are refused for up to 5 minutes), 205 (the cache outage's 5 s stall, which refused one checkout, and a warning per cache call: 300,915 in a minute at high load) and 206 (Cosmos: every failure takes about 13 s, the catalog answers 500, one checkout ended in a generic error). The runbooks were not written: the cache fallback's waits for 205, the issuer's and Cosmos's for the deployed environment (`docs/operations/runbooks/README.md`, Planned). Not run: an issuer outage longer than a token's life, a service starting during one, several catalog instances, a real Cosmos account.
Three dependency failures are described in `docs/architecture/patterns.md` section 14 as designed or read, not shown: the mock issuer stopped while a customer signs in (only the cold first request and the Android refresh-on-5xx are measured); a Cosmos outage in the middle of a request (the adapters pass against the emulator, an outage is not examined); and the cache Redis going down under load (the fallback is tested with the chaos provider and with the cache Redis unreachable at start, not with a real outage while readers are active). The event broker outage and an ordering restart have been run (`docs/evidence/runbook-event-broker-2026-10-30.md`, the 66-minute soak). Reconciled means: a run for each (stop the container, observe what the customer sees, restart, observe recovery), recorded as evidence, and the table in section 14 updated. The runbooks for the issuer and the catalog (`docs/operations/runbooks/README.md`) are the natural place to record them.

Type: Stale test tooling · Source: the browser suite run of 2026-11-09 · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-09 · option 1, chosen by the lead: the script no longer starts servers; each run is `playwright test`, and the Playwright config's `webServer` entries start the mock BFF, the fake issuer and the app with its login settings, so the script cannot drift from `npm run test:e2e`. Run with `--runs 2`: 155 of 155 passed both times, 77 to 78 s a run (the build repeats each run, from Next's cache; the earlier 31 to 34 s figures in `docs/architecture/nfrs.md` are the 2026-10-30 baseline of 62 tests and stay as that dated snapshot).
The script started the mock BFF and `next start` only. Since the login moved to the app's server (ADR-0019) the suite also needs the fake issuer on 5101 and the login settings that `apps/web-customer/playwright.config.ts` sets in its `webServer` entries. **168. Web-customer browser specs leave the mock BFF's allergy profile set, so a second run against the same mock fails**
Type: Test isolation · Source: trying to speed up `repeat-suite.mjs` by sharing servers across runs, 2026-11-09 · Hat: Engineering/Build
Status: Resolved 2026-11-09 · the five specs each have an `afterEach` that resets the mock's profile (`resetPreferences`, with `resetOrders` where the spec's own reset has it); the same 3-run trial with servers started once then passed all three runs, 155 of 155 each (78 to 80 s a run), where before runs 2 and 3 each failed the same cancel-order test. That confirms the cause, which the entry below had only read from the specs. `repeat-suite.mjs` still starts fresh servers per run (sharing saves nothing).
`checkout-allergens`, `diets`, `personalized`, `preferences` and `tag-filter` (in `apps/web-customer/tests/e2e/`) each reset the mock's customer profile (`resetPreferences`) at the start of their tests but none has an `afterEach`, so the last spec to run leaves a profile set. Within one run the file order hides it (those specs run after `cancel-order`); against the same mock a second time, `cancel-order.spec.ts` "an order that is waiting for the bakery can be cancelled too" times out (30 s) in run 2 and run 3 on the checkout page, which the leftover allergy profile holds. Shown by a 3-run trial with the servers started once (run 1 155 of 155, runs 2 and 3 154 of 155, the same test); the cause is read from the specs and the page snapshot, not proven by a fix. The trial also showed the point of sharing servers was not there: the suite takes 77 s with or without a build and start per run (the earlier 31 to 34 s were 62 tests), so `repeat-suite.mjs` keeps fresh servers per run. Reconciled means: an `afterEach` (or one global teardown) that resets the profile in those specs, and a 3-run trial with shared servers passing.

**170. Checkout answers a failing payment processor or a failing store with a bare 500, where an unreachable catalog is a 503**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing · Source: the `payment` and `repository` chaos layers, `ChaosHostTests` in the ordering tests, 2026-11-09 (`WI-CHAOS-002`) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (pushed and merged in PR 41 with all pipeline checks green; the emulator run of flows 07 and 11 is done, 2026-11-11, but those flows do not reach the payment or store outage; the lead approved the direction 2026-11-10 and the shared-library placement 2026-11-11): `PaymentUnavailableException` (on the `IPaymentProcessor` contract; the chaos layer throws it) and `StoreUnavailableException` (`BakeryMarketplace.Data`; `CosmosRepository` and `CosmosBatchFactory` convert only 503, 408 and 429, the chaos repository layer throws it) are mapped by `CheckoutService.CheckoutAsync` to the existing 503 `downstream_unavailable` after the stock is given back. A bug or a 4xx stays a 500. `ChaosHostTests` now pin the 503; `StoreUnavailableExceptionTests` pin which statuses count. The conversion was then run against the real SDK (2026-11-11, section 14 of the chaos proposal): the vNext emulator for the chaos layer over Cosmos, and a closed port for an unreachable store, which showed that the SDK lets an `HttpRequestException` through when it cannot connect; that is now converted too. Other services that use `IRepository` (catalog) still give a 500 for a store that cannot be reached: mapping them is separate.
With a chaos scenario that makes the payment processor throw, or the Cosmos store throw a 503, `POST /ordering/checkout` releases the stock reservation (correct: the `reserved` flag in `CheckoutService`) and rethrows, so the customer gets a bare 500. An unreachable catalog gets `503 downstream_unavailable` with a code the web and Android apps already handle (both treat every transport-class failure alike, so the customer-facing effect is the same generic "could not be placed" message; the difference is the status and code a client or an operator sees, and that a 500 is not marked as retryable). Options: map processor and store failures to the same `Unavailable` outcome, or leave it and document why. Pinned meanwhile by `AProcessorThatFails_...` and `AStoreThatFails_...` in `ChaosHostTests`, so a change is deliberate.

**171. Two retries the lost-response fault shows are not safe yet: a payment authorization and a notification**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing · Source: the `lostResponseRate` fault, `ChaosHostTests` and `ChaosSenderTests`, 2026-11-09 (`WI-CHAOS-003`) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-11 (the lead agreed the order of the work: (1) notifications: a copy of an event in one batch is dropped, an event is claimed atomically before it is sent (`ICacheProvider.SetIfAbsentAsync`, a 2-minute lease; one whose claim is held and not sent is handed back for another delivery, never dropped), and `INotificationSender` is idempotent on the event id (the log and recording senders honor it); (2) payments: `IPaymentProcessor` is idempotent on the order id and has an idempotent `VoidAsync`, a failed checkout voids before it releases stock, the orphan sweep voids before it releases, and the stub honors both. Whole stack, `docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-003/`: `lost-confirmation` 0 orders held twice (before 13 of 25), 46 lost notification answers and 50 repeats absorbed, 0 notifications sent twice; `flaky-broker` 0 notified twice (before 5). What remains is a promise, not a fact: a real processor (`PAY-001`) and a real email provider (`WI-NOT-002`) must keep to the two contracts, which those specs state)
(1) A payment whose answer is lost was authorized, the checkout releases the stock and gives the customer an error, and the customer's retry authorizes again: two authorizations for one order. `IPaymentProcessor` has no idempotency key and no void, so the real processor (`PAY-001`) must be called with the order id as its idempotency key and a failed checkout must void an authorization it cannot confirm. (2) A notification whose answer is lost was sent and is sent again on "try again later": nothing in the sender contract or the sent ledger (which is written after success) prevents it, so the real provider needs an idempotency key per event. Idempotency of checkout itself holds: a lost store write leaves the order stored and a replay with the same key returns it (`AStoreWriteWhoseResponseIsLost_...`). Pinned by tests so a change is deliberate. Measured on the whole stack 2026-11-11 (after 179 was fixed, so a retry reaches payment): under `lost-confirmation` 13 of 25 orders were authorized more than once; `chaos-smoke.mjs` reports it as a known gap in `summary.json`.

**172. Aimed faults, the token read and signals in the chaos library, and flow 11 on a scenario (for review)**
Type: Decision recorded and built, review · Source: the lead's approvals of 2026-11-10 (aimed faults, customer identity from the token, signals), `chaos-scenarios-proposal.md` section 12, ADR-0024 amendment · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead reviewed and closed it; pushed and merged in PR 41, pipeline green, so the clean-checkout build of the BFF and `@bakery/chaos` is confirmed) (built and unit-tested: TypeScript package 67 tests, BFF wrapper tests, C# checks 71; **run 2026-11-11 on the emulator: flow 11 passed under its scenario, with one aimed `placeOrder` failure in the BFF journal** (`docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-003/flows-07-11-2026-11-11.md`); the whole-stack scenarios do not use aimed faults; needs the lead's review)
(1) `failFirstAttempts`, `where`, `off` and `signals` in the scenario file; `chaos/layers.json` lists the sections and actions, both libraries are tested against it. (2) The BFF's client wrapper reads the `sub` of the bearer token **without verifying it** to choose which calls a rule is about; it decides nothing about access, is not logged or journaled, and the services still validate every token (an auth-adjacent read, approved by the lead). (3) A signal is a normal request matching a rule in the file; it carries no setting and cannot name a scenario or a path. It is chaos-only (the allow-list gate), journaled, and limited to the process that sees the request. This is a request-driven change of chaos state, which ADR-0024's first text avoided; the amendment says why it is safe, and it is **not a test-only product hook**: it is chaos-library behaviour that is on only where a scenario file is. (4) Test tooling: `createStack().startServices({ bffChaos })` and `mobile-flows.mjs` give the BFF the `mobile-flows` scenario at start; flow 11 no longer stops the BFF and places two Oat Milk Lattes (the order the scenario names) so that no other flow's order matches. (5) **Found while committing, fixed:** `packages/chaos` had never been committed. `.gitignore` ignores `**/packages/*` and exempted only `web-session` and `customer-core`, so commit 9607030 (WI-CHAOS-003) left the whole package out while the BFF imports `@bakery/chaos`; a clean clone or the pipeline would have failed to build the BFF. `.gitignore` now exempts `/packages/chaos/` and the package is tracked from the commit of 2026-11-10. Worth checking after the push that the pipeline's BFF and chaos steps build from a clean checkout. Counters last for the BFF's life, so a flow run twice against one running stack fails only the first time; a `reset` signal would cover that and is not used yet.

**173. Flow 07 still stops and starts the BFF**
Type: Open work · Source: the same session, 2026-11-10 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-12 (the lead reviewed and closed it; flow 07 ran again on the emulator on 2026-11-12 as part of all 15 flows passing in one run, 9m 15s, on the tree with the dependency changes of register 162; the `checkCart` rule is still not exercised by a flow, see the evidence record); pushed and merged in PR 41; built 2026-11-11, **run 2026-11-11 on the emulator: flow 07 passed with no BFF stop** (the signal switched the scenario and Nora's first profile and payment-method reads failed once each; the `checkCart` rule was not exercised, see the evidence record); to close once pushed and the lead has reviewed (decided 2026-11-10: a signal, with a **new seed listing** that only flow 07 opens). Seed listing `listing-cardamom-latte-001` ("Cardamom Honey Latte", made to order); `chaos/scenarios/mobile-flows.json` switches the BFF to `mobile-flows-07` when `get` is called for it, and that scenario fails Nora's first `getPreferences`, `checkCart` and `listPaymentMethods` (the retry of the payment methods goes through) and keeps flow 11's rule; flow 07 no longer stops the BFF (the earlier flow also proved the BFF being gone at checkout; the scenario gives the same failures to the app, and the Android equivalence test shows the app treats them alike; a real process that is gone stays flow 15's issuer restart). Tests: `chaos-calls.test.ts` runs the real files; seed tests count seven listings. Run `node scripts/dev/mobile-flows.mjs --flow 07` and `--flow 11` on the emulator before relying on it.
The calls that must fail at checkout (`listPaymentMethods`, `getPreferences`, `checkCart`) carry only the token, and flow 05 logs in as the same customer (Nora) before it, so a rule on the customer alone would fire in flow 05. The lead's decision, as in past similar cases: do not search the flows and the code for something that happens to be unique; **add a new listing to the seed data** (named for the flow, made to order so it never runs out, no effect on any other flow) so the request that opens it is unique and new by construction. That request matches a signal that enables the layer, and the layer's rule then fails Nora's first calls. Seed listings may have knock-on tests (listing counts, browse screens, the catalog's seed tests, search and tag results): find and update them in the same change. Flow 15 (the issuer) and a real restart stay process control.

**175. Change the session usage record: no wall time, relative cost instead of dollars**
Type: Process · Source: the lead, 2026-11-10 · Hat: Process
Status: Resolved 2026-11-11 (the records; the sessions of 2026-11-10 and later are entered in the new format only when the lead pastes the screen)
Decided: the usage records show a **relative cost**, the usage screen's figure divided by a base of **2.00** to two decimals (a size compared with other sessions, never money), and no wall-clock time. Reason: the dollar figure the screen shows is not an accurate measure for most users and misleads. The sessions of 2026-11-10 (1.25) and 2026-11-11 (3.39) are the first entered in the new format, in `docs/evidence/ai-usage/ai-usage-2026-11-11.md`; the series is 22 sessions, 141.12. Not changed: `docs/history/` (closed), and the assistant's own usage log in memory.

**178. Review test design: tests must not break when data or other tests are added**
Type: Process / test design · Source: the lead, 2026-11-11 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (the lead confirmed the rule; A to D and F fixed; E moved to item 200)
Progress 2026-11-17 (review of every test suite: the seed-reading and real-stack ones read in full, the rest screened for exact counts, shared state and order dependence). Findings, most serious first: (A) **shared stock on the real stack**: the seed has 12 sourdough and the skeleton specs take 11 of them per run without giving them back (journey 4, order-allergens 2, queue-allergens 2, vendor-queue 2, order-history 1, stack 1; vendor-actions and vendor-live already bring their own units), so one more spec that buys sourdough sells it out and breaks unrelated later specs; `journey.spec.ts` also leaves the croissant at 0 (the sold-out test does not restore it) and its "Low stock" check depends on what earlier files left. Fix (not made yet): each test that places orders brings the units it takes first (`adjustStock`), the sold-out test restores in `finally`, the low-stock test sets the croissant to a known low amount itself. (B) **exact seed lists**: `walking-skeleton.spec.ts` lines 25 and 46 (count of published listings, the original incident, still exact), `listing-tags.spec.ts` 82 to 84 and 99 (the vegan filter's exact result), `stack.spec.ts` 131 and `journey.spec.ts` 136 (Alice's payment methods exactly), and in .NET `SeederTests` (7 listings), `SeedCustomerProfilesTests` (4 profiles), `SeedPaymentMethodsTests` (Alice's ids). The three .NET ones are FIXED (counted from `SeedData`/`PaymentSeedData`; 12 and 7 tests pass); the Playwright ones are not. (C) **mock fixture counts**: the web app's mock-backed suite asserts the three listings of fixture A by count (`toHaveCount(3)` and filtered counts in `ssr-relay`, `personalized`, `diets`, `tag-filter`, about 20 assertions); a new mock listing breaks them. Proposed: read the count from the mock's own GraphQL (`listings`) or assert by name. (D) **cleanup inside the test body**: skeleton `personalized.spec.ts` and `preferences.spec.ts` clear Alice's profile at the end of each test, so a failure leaves it set for later files; move to `afterEach`. (E) Android flows 05 and 10 share the croissant's 4 units by a written budget (low; a fresh stack per run; a new seeded listing per flow would remove it). (F) `CosmosRepositoryTests` used a fixed database name; FIXED (its own name per test, as the other emulator tests; builds; not run against the emulator). Kept on purpose: the closed allergen list of ADR-0022 (a contract, not seed data), the guard `Nobody_else_can_hit_a_decline_by_accident`, the queue display's suite (each test sets its own orders, the model to follow), the chaos specs (own data by rule), unit tests with their own fakes, Redis and Cosmos tests named per test. **Decision for the lead:** how new seed data is announced to the specs that read the seed (proposed: specs name seed data points by id and never enumerate the seed; seed tests count from `SeedData`; a test that needs something unique adds a new named seed data point).
Found when adding the seed listing `listing-cardamom-latte-001` (for flow 07) broke two Playwright tests in `walking-skeleton.spec.ts`, which asserted an exact count of seeded public listings (a hard-coded list of 6). The unit tests had been updated; the browser spec was missed until a whole-stack run, and the fix was to extend the list. The rule the lead set: a test must not fail because a new test or new seed data was added. To do later: review all existing tests (.NET, Node, Playwright, Maestro) and the test design patterns for assertions on exact seed counts or lists, shared mutable state between tests, and order dependence; prefer asserting on the data a test creates or names, or on "contains" rather than "equals all". Also decide how new seed data is announced to the specs that read the seed. Related: chaos specs are added per scenario in their own folder and the default suite runs with no scenario (the lead, 2026-11-11).
Update 2026-11-17, later (the lead: resume 178): the Playwright fixes are made, following the proposals above. (A) Each real-stack test that places an order first brings the units it takes (`bringStock` in `tests/skeleton/support.ts`: journey, order-allergens, queue-allergens, vendor-queue, order-history, stack); the two croissant tests in `journey.spec.ts` set the amount they need (`setStock`: 3, which is low, then 0 for the sold-out test) and put back what they found in `finally`. (B) No real-stack spec enumerates the seed: `walking-skeleton.spec.ts` checks that each named listing is shown, not the count; the tag filter checks that every listing in the answer carries every tag asked for and names the seeded listings that must be in or out; Alice's payment methods are named, not listed (journey, stack); the empty list of the customer seeded with no methods stays exact, since that is what that seed data point is for. (C) The mock-backed suite counts from the mock's own fixtures (`FIXTURE_A`, `fixtureWithTags`, `fixtureWithItemAllergen`, `hiddenLine` in `tests/e2e/helpers.ts`, typed by `mock-bff/fixtures.d.mts`) in `ssr-relay`, `personalized`, `diets` and `tag-filter`; conflict counts on a named listing are kept, they are about that listing. (D) `personalized.spec.ts` and `preferences.spec.ts` (skeleton) clear Alice's profile in `afterEach`. Evidence: typecheck, lint and format pass; the four mock-backed specs 36 of 36 in Chromium, and 36 of 36 again with a fourth listing added to fixture A for the run (then removed); `node scripts/ci/skeleton-smoke.mjs` 62 of 62 on a fresh stack. Not run: a second smoke on a kept stack, the Android flows. Left: (E), the Android flows' shared croissant budget (low). For the lead: confirm the rule the fixes follow, so it can be written into `docs/testing.md`: specs name seed data points by id and never enumerate the seed; seed tests and mock-backed tests count from the seed or the fixture; a test that changes shared data brings or restores it itself; a test that needs something unique adds a new named seed data point.
Resolution 2026-11-17: the lead confirmed the rule; it is working rule 6 in `docs/testing.md`. (E) is item 200.

**179. A checkout retried with the same idempotency key cannot recover after the stock was given back**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing (needs a decision; checkout and catalog business logic) · Source: `tests/chaos/checkout-trouble.spec.ts` under `checkout-trouble`, 2026-11-11 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-11 (the lead chose option (b): `StockReservationService.ReserveAsync` revives a released reservation for the SAME lines, in place and in the same batch as the stock decrements, with a new creation time for the orphan sweep and no expiry; different lines are still refused with 409 `reservation_released`. Tests: `AReleasedReservationIsReservedAgainForTheSameLines`, `AReleasedReservationIsNotReservedAgainForDifferentLines`; whole stack: `checkout-trouble` 30 of 30 orders confirmed, the recovery spec 12 of 12). Not covered: a late duplicate release arriving after a revive would release the newer reservation (a release carries no generation); the gateway releases inside the checkout call, so no such release is known to arrive after the retry.
When a checkout fails after the stock was reserved (a declined payment, or a failure it cleans up), it releases the reservation. The reservation id is the order id, which is derived from the customer and the idempotency key, so a retry with the **same key** finds the released reservation; the catalog keeps it 30 days (`StockReservationDocument.ReleasedRetention`) and refuses to reuse it (409 `reservation_released`), which checkout maps to `CONFLICT` ("the stock changed, please try again"). Trying again can never succeed in that window. In the run: 36 of 83 attempts were `CONFLICT`, 6 of 30 orders never succeeded in 8 attempts, while the journal showed 3 injected conflicts. A client that gets a new key for each retry is not affected; the Android flows and any client that retries "the same order" with the same key are. Options (not decided): a checkout attempt number in the reservation id; allowing a released reservation to be reserved again for the same lines (a late release must still not undo a newer reserve); or answering `CONFLICT` with a message that says a new key is needed. Related: 171 (a payment authorized and lost is authorized again) is masked by this, because a retry never reaches the payment while 179 holds.

**180. An order whose store write succeeded but whose answer was lost has its stock given back**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing (needs a decision; checkout business logic) · Source: `tests/chaos/lost-confirmation.spec.ts` under `lost-confirmation`, 2026-11-11 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-11 (the lead agreed to the direction: `CheckoutService` looks the order up by id before releasing and releases only when the order is known not to exist; when the lookup itself fails the reservation is kept and the orphan sweep returns it if there is no order. Tests: `AStoreWriteWhoseResponseIsLost_...` now also asserts the reservation is kept; whole stack: `lost-confirmation` 30 of 30 confirmed with stock down by exactly 30)
`CheckoutService` releases the reservation in `catch (Exception) when (reserved)` when `TryCreateAsync` throws, including a `StoreUnavailableException` raised by a lost response after the write succeeded. The order then exists (a retry with the same key returns it) but its stock was released: in the run 19 orders were confirmed and the stock fell by 14, so stock can be sold twice. The earlier host test (`AStoreWriteWhoseResponseIsLost_...`) checks that the replay returns the order, not the stock. Direction (not decided): after a failed create, look the order up by id before releasing; release only when it does not exist.

**182. A broker outage parks the outboxes of healthy orders, and a parked order never publishes its events**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing (needs a decision; outbox semantics, related to 74) · Source: `tests/chaos/broker-outage.spec.ts` under `broker-outage`, 2026-11-11 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-11 (whole stack: `broker-outage` 45 of 45 confirmed orders reached the vendor queue and got their notification, 121 outage faults injected, nothing parked; the lead agreed to the direction. The event adapters raise a typed `EventTransportException` (Redis: connection and timeout failures; the chaos layer: outage, failure, lost response); `OrderedPublishOutcome.CountsTowardParking` is false for it, so neither the ordering nor the catalog outbox counts or parks on a transport failure; `OutboxRelayBase` backs off while sweeps find events but send none. Tests: `OutboxTests.ABrokerOutageNeverParksTheOrder...`, `OutboxFailureKindTests`; the `broker-outage` spec is no longer an expected failure.
`OrderEventPublisher` counted each failed publish and at `Outbox:MaxPublishAttempts` (5) parks the order's outbox: it is no longer retried until an operator unparks it (register 74, built for an event that can never succeed). A transient broker outage fails five attempts within seconds, so it parks every order placed in or just before it. In the run (event outage 5 s to 30 s): 45 orders were accepted and readable by their customers, but 11 never reached the vendor queue and never got an `OrderPlaced` notification, still missing 90 s after the outage ended (the vendor's screen never shows them). Direction for the lead (not decided): count only failures that cannot succeed (a payload that does not serialize, an unknown event type) toward parking, and retry a failure of the transport (broker unreachable, timeout) without limit, on the relay's sweep with a back-off; the transport failures are told apart by a typed exception from the event adapters, as in 170.

**183. A notification event is dead-lettered after 3 failed deliveries and nothing replays it**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing (to decide) · Source: `flaky-broker` run, 2026-11-11 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Tracking (direction agreed 2026-11-11: retry with back-off, no replay job for now), built: `RedisStreamsEventTransport` redelivers after the redeliver time (30 s, then 60 s for every later failure) and gives up after 10 deliveries instead of 3 (about 9 minutes). A replay of the dead-letter stream stays an operator follow-up if a handler can be down longer. Whole stack: `flaky-broker` 30 of 30 confirmed orders notified, nothing dead-lettered (before: 1 lost), queue converged in 51 s; summary in `docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-003/`.)
With the event handler failing 30% of the time (`flaky-broker`), 1 of 30 confirmed orders never got its `OrderPlaced` notification even after waiting minutes: its event failed 3 deliveries and was moved to the dead-letter stream (the log says so), and nothing reads that stream. A real handler outage (the contact directory down, say) lasting a few redeliveries has the same effect. Needs a decision: a replay of the dead-letter stream (a job or an operator command), or more deliveries with a back-off before giving up. The vendor queue converged in the same run (its consumer did not exhaust its deliveries).

**184. A failed cache invalidation can make the old status come back within the TTL (the breaker and the fallback)**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing (design question, for the lead) · Source: `stale-cache` and a hand probe, 2026-11-11 (`WI-CHAOS-003`) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-18 (the lead chose the pending delete, options (b) and (c) together; see the resolution below) · was: Open · documented, not changed (the lead asked on 2026-11-11 for the small gap to be fixed and this one documented). The other half is fixed: `ResilientCacheProvider.DeleteAsync` now always deletes from the fallback too, so a successful invalidation no longer leaves a stale copy in the fallback (test `ASuccessfulDeleteAlsoRemovesTheCopyTheFallbackHolds`).
`ResilientCacheProvider` counts a failed delete as a primary failure. A failed delete leaves the primary's old entry in place (the primary is Redis, so the entry lives there until its TTL); three failures in a row open the breaker for 30 s, during which reads come from the fallback and the store and are fresh; when the breaker closes, reads go to the primary again and the old entry is served again until its TTL. So within the 60 s TTL after a change a customer can see old, then new, then old, then new. The bound ADR-0002 accepts still holds (an entry is never served more than the TTL after it was loaded, so never longer than the TTL after a change; shown by `stale-cache`: the old status for 59.6 s, the new one from 60.1 s). The flap itself has been seen only in part (the fresh reads while the breaker was open, in a probe); the old entry returning after the breaker closes is expected from the code and not observed. What it costs: a status shown wrongly for up to 60 s, never an oversold item (checkout's atomic reservation decides, ADR-0017); cart and order data are never cached. Options if it matters: (a) accept it, as the ADR does for staleness (this item and an ADR-0002 note are the record); (b) after a failed delete, remember the key in the instance and bypass the primary for it until its TTL (fixes it for one instance, not across instances; more code in the resilient provider); (c) retry the delete a few times before giving up (shortens the window when Redis recovers quickly, does nothing for a longer outage).
Resolution 2026-11-18: `ResilientCacheProvider` keeps a pending-delete mark per key whose primary delete failed (refused by the open breaker or failed by the primary). Every later call for that key (get, get-many, set, set-if-absent, exists) first tries the delete again through the breaker; while it still fails the call uses the fallback only, counted as `cache_fallback` with reason `pending_delete`; when it lands the mark is cleared and the key goes to the primary, whose old entry is now gone for every instance. A successful delete clears any earlier mark; a mark is cleared by a retry only if no newer failed delete replaced it. The marks are per instance (another instance can read the old entry within the TTL until this one's delete lands) and last until a delete reaches the primary. Tests: `ResilientCacheProviderTests` (the flap itself, with a breaker opened and closed by hand: a miss after the close, not the old value; every operation uses the fallback while the delete fails and other keys still use the primary; a later delete clears the mark) and `ChaosCacheProviderTests.ADeleteFailureBehindTheResilientProviderLeavesTheOldValueInThePrimaryButDoesNotServeIt` (was `...StillLeavesTheOldValueServed`, which pinned the old behaviour). The `stale-cache` spec now requires that the old status never comes back after the new one and no longer requires a stale sample (the journal count shows the injection). Whole stack, `node scripts/ci/chaos-smoke.mjs stale-cache` (`docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-003/summary-stale-cache-2026-11-18.json`): 6 failed deletes injected, the new status from the first sample, 0 of 176 samples stale (were 117, new from 60.1 s). ADR-0002 has a dated note. Not run: two catalog instances sharing one Redis (the per-instance limit is reasoned, not measured).

**171 (addendum 2026-11-11).** The same run shows the notification half too: with duplicate delivery and failing handlers 3 to 5 events of 62 were sent as a notification twice (delivery is at-least-once, the sent ledger is written after the send); reported as a known gap in `summary.json`. The log sender sends nothing real, so no customer is affected today.

**187. The by-hand steps of the local-development page have not been repeated in a fresh clone since 2026-10-30**
Type: Verification gap · Source: the pre-publish review, gap 9 (2026-11-08); moved here 2026-11-16 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-16
Resolution: "Run slice 1" was followed step by step in a fresh clone of `feature/public-review` (`c59ec8e`). Steps 1 to 6 and the web build passed as written; step 7's login failed: 503 because the page never read `SESSION_ENCRYPTION_KEY` from `.env`, and 500 with the key but without the sessions Redis, which no step started. With both, the login answered 302 to the issuer. The page now starts the `with-redis` profile in step 2 and reads the key in step 7, its status line and its "What this was run against" section record the run, and the seed's listing count and a test count were replaced by wording that does not go stale. Not repeated in a second fresh clone after the fix; the 500 is item 192.

**191. A session-package test failed once in the full test report and its name was not kept**
Type: Defect (intermittent test or test tooling) · Source: `node scripts/ci/test-report.mjs --write` on 2026-11-16, commit `ed95444` (`docs/evidence/test-and-coverage-2026-11-16.md`) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (closed by the lead: the failure did not recur in two full runs or eight reruns, and the report now names any failure and keeps its output)
In the full run (Redis and the Cosmos emulator up, every .NET suite just before it) `packages/web-session` reported 190 tests, 189 passed, 1 failed. Nothing on that branch touches the package. The suite then passed in eight reruns against Redis (three plain, five with the report's coverage flags), so the failure looks load-dependent. The report keeps only the counts of a `node --test` suite, not the runner's output, so which test failed is not known. Reconciled when the report saves the runner's output of a failing suite under `artifacts/` (test tooling, adds nothing to the product), and the test is found and made robust or the failure is shown not to recur in repeated full runs.
Update 2026-11-16: the first half is done. `scripts/ci/test-report.mjs` keeps each `node --test` suite's whole output in `artifacts/coverage/js/<suite>.log`, reads the names of failed .NET tests from their TRX files, and writes a "Failed tests" section into the record (suite, test names, where the output is). Checked with a test made to fail on purpose (named in the record, then deleted) and against a real TRX file. The second half waits for the next full run.

**192. The web login answers 500 when the sessions Redis cannot be reached**
Type: Defect · Source: the fresh-clone run of the local-development page, 2026-11-16 (item 187) · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-16 (the lead approved the change the same day)
Resolution: the Redis session store turns a failure to reach the server into `SessionStoreUnavailableError` (`packages/web-session/src/store.ts`; an error reply from Redis, or a record that cannot be opened, stays what it was). The login and callback routes answer it as they answer a rate limit: a redirect to `/auth/error?code=unavailable`, with the fixed sentence "Login is not available just now. Please try again in a moment.", and a warning in the server log; the callback does not exchange the code. A redirect rather than a bare 503, because both routes are browser navigations and the rate-limit answer set the precedent; anything else still fails as before. Tests: the real Redis adapter pointed at a port where nothing listens (every operation raises the new error), and the handlers with a store that cannot be reached and with one that fails otherwise (`tests/stores.test.ts`, `tests/handlers.test.ts`; 194 of 194 in the package). Run on the built customer app with every login setting and the sessions Redis down: `GET /api/auth/login` answered 302 to `/auth/error?code=unavailable` (was 500); after starting the sessions Redis the same process answered 302 to the issuer's `/authorize`, without a restart. Not checked by hand in a browser. The other routes are item 194.
With every login setting given but the sessions Redis (port 6381) not running, `GET /api/auth/login` of the customer app answered 500. A missing or bad setting answers 503 and logs the setting's name (`apps/web-customer/README.md`); an unreachable store is the same kind of condition (try again later), and the queue display shares the code (`packages/web-session`). Reconciled when the login routes answer 503 when the session store cannot be reached, with a test, or the 500 is shown to be intended and the README says so. Session handling is auth code: the change needs the lead's approval.

**193. Two event-transport failure tests checked the log before the transport had written it**
Type: Defect (intermittent test) · Source: the pull-request pipeline of `feature/public-review` (build 63, `.NET build and tests`, "Test shared libraries"), 2026-11-16 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-16
`RedisFailureReportingTests.AHandlerThatThrowsIsReportedAndTheEntryIsDeadLetteredAtTheLimit` failed on the Linux agent: both "handler failed" warnings were logged, the "dead-lettered" error was not. The transport writes the dead-letter entry, then acknowledges it, then logs; the test waited only for the dead-letter entry and then checked the log, so on a fast agent it could look between the write and the log line. `AnEnvelopeThatIsNotJsonIsDeadLetteredWithTheReasonAndReported` had the same gap for its pending count and its log line. Nothing on the branch touched the transport or these tests: the race was old and rare (the same tree passed `linux-check.mjs`).
Resolution: both tests now wait for everything they assert (the dead-letter entry, nothing pending, the log line), and the assertions stay so a failure still names what is missing. The transport is unchanged: logging before the write would report a dead-letter that might not happen. Checked by delaying the two log calls by 300 ms in a scratch change to the transport (reverted): the old tests failed in both places, the new ones passed; then 10 of 10 runs of the eventing tests against the Compose Redis without the delay. The other log assertions in the .NET tests check after an awaited call returns and do not have this race.

**194. With the sessions Redis down, the session routes other than login still answer 500, and every reconnect is logged as an unhandled error**
Type: Defect · Source: the fix of item 192, 2026-11-16 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-16 (approved by the lead the same day)
Resolution: `me`, `logout`, `graphql` and `graphql-stream` go through one guard (`whenStoreUnreachable` in `packages/web-session/src/handlers.ts`): `SessionStoreUnavailableError` becomes 503 with `Retry-After: 5`, `no-store`, the fixed sentence `STORE_UNAVAILABLE_MESSAGE` and a warning in the log; any other error, or one after the answer has started, fails as before. An open stream's session checks already tolerated failures and are unchanged. The Redis client has an `error` listener: the first failure is logged by its code (never the URL, which may carry a password), then at most one in 30 seconds, and one line when the server is back. The apps' pages need no change: a failed `me` already shows the login, and a failed GraphQL call already shows "could not load" with Try again. Tests: the guard on each route and its pass-through of other errors, and the listener against a closed port (one log line, nothing printed by the client); 196 of 196 in the package. Run on the built customer app with the sessions Redis down: `me` and `graphql` answered 503 with the sentence, the login 302 to the error page, and the log held one "cannot be reached (ECONNREFUSED)" line instead of a line per reconnect; after starting Redis, `me` answered 200 and the log said "can be reached again".
Item 192 mapped an unreachable sessions Redis for the login and callback routes only (its scope). `/api/auth/me`, `/api/auth/logout`, `/api/graphql` and `/api/graphql-stream` ask the store about the session cookie and still fail with a 500 when it cannot be reached; `me` is asked on every page load. Separately, the Redis client has no `error` listener, so while Redis is down ioredis prints "[ioredis] Unhandled error event: ... ECONNREFUSED" to the server log on every reconnect attempt (seen in the customer app's log 2026-11-16, and in the package's tests). Options: answer `SessionStoreUnavailableError` with a 503 "try again" in those routes (one place: the `route` wrapper in `runtime.ts`, or each handler), and give the client an `error` listener that reports the first failure and then at most one in 30 seconds (as the C# event transport does). Session handling is auth code: the change needs the lead's approval. Reconciled when built or declined.

**195. During a session-store outage a logged-in customer is asked to log in, and the login then says it is not available**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing · Source: the `session-outage` run, 2026-11-16 (`WI-CHAOS-004`, `docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-004/README.md`) · Hat: Product/UX
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (approved by the lead)
Resolution: both web apps have a fourth session state, `unavailable`, set only when `me` answers 503 (item 194). A page that needs a login shows "Login is not available just now. Please try again in a moment." with Try again (`login-unavailable`, `RequireLogin.tsx`), instead of the login button; Try again asks `me` again. Any other failed answer is still "nobody". The `session-outage` spec now requires the new notice during the outage and that Try again brings the earlier session back without a new login; see `docs/evidence/WI-CHAOS-004/README.md` for the run.
While the session store cannot be reached, `GET /api/auth/me` answers 503 (item 194). Both web apps treat any failed answer from `me` as "nobody is logged in" (`src/lib/session.ts`, `refreshSession`), so a customer who is logged in sees the login prompt; pressing it lands on "Login is not available just now". Nothing is lost: the session is kept and the page shows the customer again once the store is back. But the first message is wrong ("log in" for "try again in a moment"), and a customer may think they were logged out. Options: let the pages tell 503 from "nobody" (a third session state that shows "Your login cannot be checked just now; try again" and no login button), or accept it and say so in the apps' READMEs. A client change in both apps' session code: the lead's decision. Reconciled when built or accepted.

**196. The web apps' network functions have no timeout: a web server that does not answer leaves the page loading**
Type: Behaviour found by chaos testing · Source: the equivalence contract of `WI-CHAOS-005`, 2026-11-16 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (approved by the lead)
Resolution: both network functions (`browserFetch`, `fetchGraphQL`) abort a call after `GRAPHQL_TIMEOUT_MS` (35 s, longer than the route's own 30 s wait for the BFF) with a `TimeoutError`, which the pages show as any other failure, with Try again. The shared contract (`packages/web-session/tests/relay-network-contract.ts`) checks it with the clock of `setTimeout` under the test's control: still waiting 1 ms before the limit, failed at it, and not taken for the end of the login (8 tests per app). The customer app's server-side rendering uses the same function and gets the same limit.
The browser's Relay network function of each web app (`browserFetch` in `apps/web-customer`, `fetchGraphQL` in `apps/web-queue-display`) calls `fetch` with no time limit. A BFF that does not answer is covered by the web route, which gives up after `UPSTREAM_TIMEOUT_MS` (30 s) and answers an error; but a web server that does not answer (a hung process, or the `web` layer's `hangRate`) leaves the page's spinner up until the connection drops (the injected hang drops it after 120 s; a real one may not). The Android app's client has a timeout (`BFF_TIMEOUT_MS`, `apps/mobile-customer/src/timeout.ts`). The contract test shows the injected hang and a real hung server alike ("nothing yet" after 1.5 s), so a change shows in it. Options: an `AbortSignal.timeout` in both network functions, longer than the route's 30 s (so a slow checkout is not cut short), which turns a hang into the same failure and Try again as a reset; or accept it. Reconciled when built or accepted.

**197. A chaos test checked an outage window against the wall clock and failed once in the CPU-limited Linux container**
Type: Defect (intermittent test) · Source: `node scripts/ci/linux-check.mjs` on 2026-11-17, commit `a36c0e5` · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17
`CoreTests.TheOutageWindowOpensAndClosesOnTheScenarioClock` (`tools/tests/BakeryMarketplace.Chaos.Tests`) slept 220 ms and 350 ms with `Thread.Sleep` around a window from 0.15 s to 0.40 s; in the limited Linux container one run fell outside it (70 of 71 passed; all 71 on Windows). Nothing in the chaos library had changed. Resolution: the test drives the runtime's `TimeProvider` by hand (`ForTests` already took one) and checks the edges exactly (closed 1 ms before 0.15 s, open at it, open 1 ms before 0.40 s, closed at it). No other .NET test calls `Thread.Sleep`. 71 of 71 on Windows and in two later `linux-check.mjs` runs (the second passed in full).

**198. A broadcast chaos test failed once in the Linux check, and the check had dropped the failure message**
Type: Defect (intermittent test, and test tooling) · Source: `node scripts/ci/linux-check.mjs` on 2026-11-17, between the runs of item 197 · Hat: Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-17 (the lead: fix it with a manual clock; all four wall-clock latency tests, not only this one)
`ChaosBroadcastTests.Latency_DelaysThePublish` (`tools/tests/BakeryMarketplace.Eventing.Tests`) failed once in the 2-CPU container while the whole eventing suite ran (122 of 123); the run took 368 ms, so its five-second wait did not time out. Which assertion failed is not known: the check's output filter kept the test's name and dropped xUnit's message.
