Missing from any spec: the tenant and who creates it (IAM, the lead's approval), the app registrations (web, the vendor app, the Android app's redirect, the services' API and roles), how Vendor and Admin roles are assigned, the cost, and how the dev stack switches from the mock (`issuer_mode` exists). Reconciled when the work item is written and the lead has reviewed it.

**223. Inside the dev environment the apps talk plain http; TLS ends at the public ingress**
Type: Known limitation · Source: the lead's question about certificates, 2026-11-20 · Hat: Security, Platform Engineering
Status: Open
The two public apps (the web app and the mock issuer) are served over https on the environment's default domain with the certificate Azure manages for it; there is no custom domain and no certificate of our own. Not yet seen serving a real app (the first apply ran the placeholder image). Inside the environment the web app calls the BFF and the BFF the services over plain http on their app names (`http://catalog`), and the Redis apps take plain TCP with no password (accepted for dev, `docs/architecture/secrets-and-sensitive-values.md` decision 5). The bearer tokens and the Cosmos connection string therefore cross the environment's internal network unencrypted. Options to weigh before any environment beyond the demo: the Container Apps environment's peer-to-peer encryption setting (to verify that the provider supports it and what it costs), TLS and a password on Redis, and a custom domain with its own certificate where a stable address is needed. Reconciled when the lead accepts this for dev and the option for later environments is chosen.

**224. The seed may write to the deployed dev environment, behind a named switch**
Type: Decision (security: widens a deliberate guard) · Source: the lead, 2026-11-20 (the post-deploy smoke needs seeded data; `WI-DEP-001`) · Hat: Security, Platform Engineering
Status: Open · decided by the lead 2026-11-20; built, not yet run in Azure
The seed (`tools/BakeryMarketplace.Seed`) refused every Cosmos endpoint but a loopback one. A deployed dev environment starts empty, and the seed cannot run from the pipeline agent (its `VendorVerified` events go to the events Redis, which is internal to the Container Apps environment), so it runs as a job inside the environment. **The rule (`SeedTarget`):** a loopback endpoint is the local emulator, unchanged; anything else is refused unless all three hold: `Seed__AllowDeployedDev` is exactly `true`, `Deployment__Environment` is exactly `dev` (the name only the dev stack sets, as for the chaos guard), and the endpoint is an https Azure Cosmos account address (one account label before `.documents.azure.com`, port 443). In the deployed case the seed creates nothing (it checks that the dev stack's containers exist) and needs the Redis address set. The switch alone, the name alone, another environment name, plain http, another host or another port are each refused (`SeedTargetTests`). Same pattern as the mock issuer's named switch (item 102). Reconciled when the seed job has run in dev and the record shows the data it wrote.

**225. `terraform-checks.mjs` fails its init step on a machine that has run a real `terraform init`**
Type: Defect in local tooling · Source: the seed job change, 2026-11-20 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Open
After a real init against the remote state (the lead's applies, 2026-11-20), each stack's git-ignored `.terraform/terraform.tfstate` holds the backend settings. `node scripts/ci/terraform-checks.mjs` runs `init -backend=false` in the pinned Terraform image, which still reads those settings and tries to sign in to the state storage with the Azure CLI, which the image does not have: `Error: unable to build authorizer for Storage API ... exec: "az": executable file not found`. Validate and the mocked tests still ran and passed. Worked around on 2026-11-20 by moving the two files aside for the run and back afterwards (all 7 steps passed). The pull-request pipeline is not affected (a clean checkout). Options: the script runs with its own `TF_DATA_DIR` inside the container, so a local real init never meets the checks; or it says what to move when it sees the file. Reconciled when the script no longer fails on such a machine.

**226. Behind the Container Apps ingress the mock issuer is expected to advertise http addresses in its discovery document**
Type: Defect (expected; read from the code, not yet seen) · Source: writing the deployed suite's issuer test, 2026-11-20 · Hat: Security, Platform Engineering
Status: Open · fix approved by the lead and built 2026-11-20; open until a deployed run shows it
**Update 2026-11-20:** approved by the lead; `ASPNETCORE_FORWARDEDHEADERS_ENABLED=true` is set on the mock-oidc app (`infra/environments/dev/main.tf`, pinned by a mocked Terraform test). Checked locally with the mock issuer's image and a request carrying `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`: without the setting the discovery document named `http://…/.well-known/jwks.json` and `http://…/token` (the defect, now seen, not only read), with it `https://` for both.
`MapMockOidcIssuer` builds `jwks_uri`, `authorization_endpoint` and `token_endpoint` from the request's scheme and host (`tools/BakeryMarketplace.Auth/MockOidcIssuer.cs`; only `issuer` is fixed by `MockOidc__Issuer`). The ingress ends TLS and forwards plain http with `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`, and the mock host does not apply forwarded headers, so the deployed discovery document is expected to name `http://mock-oidc.<domain>/...`. The services refuse plain-http metadata outside local development (item 176), so fetching the signing keys would fail and every authenticated call would be refused; the web app's server would send its token request to an http address the ingress redirects. Not caught before: no test ran the issuer behind a TLS-ending proxy. The deployed suite's test 3 now checks that every discovery address is the https issuer's. **Proposed fix:** set `ASPNETCORE_FORWARDEDHEADERS_ENABLED=true` on the mock-oidc app in the dev stack (ASP.NET Core then takes the scheme and client address from `X-Forwarded-Proto` and `X-Forwarded-For`); a mocked Terraform test pins it. What it trusts: the header from any caller. From outside, only the ingress reaches the app; an app inside the environment that sent its own header would only change the addresses in its own answer. The alternative, building the addresses from `MockOidc__Issuer`, breaks the local Compose stack (the services there reach the issuer by its container name, not the browser's address). Reconciled when the lead decides, and a deployed run shows https addresses in the discovery document and a login that works.

**227. An apply that times out on a create can leave the resource in Azure but outside the state; the next apply and the teardown then fail on it**
Type: Risk (seen once) · Source: the first full dev deploy run, 2026-11-20 (`docs/evidence/WI-DEP-001/first-full-dev-runs-2026-11-20.md`) · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-21 (build 88 created the environment in 14 min 43 s, inside the limit) · was: Open · the create limit was raised and the runbook updated on 2026-11-20 (lead's go-ahead); the cause is not known
Azure accepted the create of the Container Apps environment and left it `Waiting` for more than 77 minutes. The provider stopped polling at its default 30 minutes (`context deadline exceeded`) and recorded nothing, so the environment was in Azure but not in the state. In that position the next apply would fail because the name is taken. The destroy cannot see the resource, so the teardown's emptiness check fails on it (it did, correctly). The lead approved deleting it by hand; it took about 25 minutes to go. No service health event was listed, the same definition had been created before without trouble, and East US had refused a Cosmos account for "high demand" the same day, so capacity is a guess.
**Update 2026-11-21 (activity log):** the create did end, `Succeeded` about 77 minutes after it was accepted, a minute before the delete; a normal create of the same definition took 14.5 minutes. The 45-minute limit allows a slow create of that kind (the apps then add about a minute), not one like this run's.

**Done:** the environment's create limit is 45 minutes, so a slow create can finish while Terraform still stops inside the apply job's 60-minute limit and releases the lock. If the job were killed mid-apply, the lock would stay held and nothing would be written to the state. The teardown runbook names this leftover and how to remove it.

**Not done, for the lead to decide if it recurs:**
1. Move the environment and the apps to another region; East US 2 already holds Cosmos.
2. Add a step after a failed apply that lists what is in `rg-bakery-dev` but not in the state, so the leftover is named in the run that made it rather than found by the next one.
3. Import a leftover that did finish creating (`terraform import`, from the lead's sign-in) instead of deleting it. That is a state change outside the pipeline's plan and approval.

Also not explained: the deploy identity went on reading the environment about every 15 seconds for about 17 minutes after the apply step ended.

Reconciled when a full deploy run creates the environment within the limit, or the lead picks one of the options above.

**Resolved 2026-11-21:** build 88 (the first complete dev deploy, `docs/evidence/WI-DEP-001/first-full-dev-deploy-2026-11-21.md`) created the environment in 14 min 43 s in East US, unchanged. The cause of the 77-minute create is still not known; if it recurs, the three options above are where to start.

**228. The deployed apps start from zero one at a time, and a cold start takes about 20 to 45 seconds an app**
Type: Finding (measured) · Source: the deployed suite from the lead's machine, 2026-11-21 (`docs/evidence/WI-DEP-001/first-full-dev-deploy-2026-11-21.md`) · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Resolved 2026-11-21 (the lead: keep scale to zero, add a warm-up step, measure the cold starts) · was: Open
The five apps scale to zero when idle (ADR-0020, decision 7). Each wakes only when something calls it, so after an idle period the first request wakes the web app, which then wakes the BFF, which then wakes catalog: the first page took 3 minutes. Run against an idle environment, the deployed suite failed three tests on cold starts (the issuer's first answer over the 30-second request limit, and the pickup windows twice while ordering started); warm, the same suite passed. The pipeline's own smoke had passed because it runs straight after the deploy has started every app.

**Decided by the lead (2026-11-21):** for a demo, scaling to zero is right and stays. The cold starts are worth measuring, so: when they happen and how long they take.

**Done:** `scripts/ci/deployed-warm-up.mjs` wakes the apps one at a time in the order they call each other (issuer, web, BFF, catalog, ordering) and records when each step started, how long until its first good answer, and every attempt. It is the deployed suite's global setup (`apps/web-customer/tests/deployed/warm-up.ts`), so no run of the suite skips it, from the pipeline (`DEPLOYED_WHERE=agent`) or the lead's machine (`DEPLOYED_WHERE=lead`); its record is `warm-up.json` with the suite's evidence and its table goes to the step's log. Run from the lead's machine on 2026-11-21; not yet run by the pipeline. `scripts/ci/dev-observe.mjs` adds the platform's side, `coldStarts` (every replica start in the window: from the platform assigning the replica to its container starting), and takes `--since <time>` for a window the lead reads from their machine. Not test-only functionality: it calls the public addresses the suite already calls, and changes nothing in the apps.

First numbers (the evidence file): the client's warm-up 20 to 26 seconds an app, 112 seconds in all; the platform's replica starts about 40 seconds at 16:50 and about 17 seconds at 17:08 for the same images (not explained).

**229. UAT next to DEV: the lead's direction, and what it changes**
Type: Decision needed · Source: the lead, 2026-11-21 · Hat: Platform Engineering
Status: Open
The lead wants a UAT environment deployed next to DEV (with Azure trial credits), and more of the full tests and chaos running in the deployed environments.

**Decided by the lead (2026-11-21):** UAT has its own Cosmos account, not on the free tier (`cosmos_free_tier = false`). The free tier is one account per subscription and stays with DEV. Alternatives weighed and not taken: the Cosmos emulator as a container app in DEV (DEV would no longer test the real adapter, and its data would go on every restart), and the in-memory store (`Data:Backend=fake`: one store per replica, emptied on every scale to zero, and the seed job cannot reach it). At 400 RU/s shared, a non-free account is about 0.03 USD an hour while it exists.

**Open, for a proposal the lead approves before anything is built:**
1. ADR-0020 decision 9 says UAT and Prod are designed and never run. Deploying UAT changes that: a dated amendment, the lead's approval.
2. There is no `infra/environments/uat` root yet: the same modules as DEV, running as Production (no chaos, ADR-0024), its own state key and resource group.
3. A deploy identity scoped to the UAT group (an IAM change), an Azure DevOps environment with its approval check, and a budget alert.
4. Promotion by digest from a recorded passing DEV smoke, as ADR-0020 already designs, and which subscription the trial credits are on.

**Update 2026-11-21, later:** the lead decided: the two refactors approved; UAT has its own state container; seeding UAT is a per-run option; the mock issuer is extended to UAT only for side-by-side runs and switched on per run; one subscription, and since it allows one Container Apps environment per region (Free Trial, read 2026-11-21), UAT's environment goes in East US 2; DEV stays up for the dev chaos work (`WI-CHAOS-006`). Run options become pipeline inputs (the table in `WI-DEP-002`). Open questions are in the spec.

The proposal is `WI-DEP-002` (written 2026-11-21). It found that two pieces of decision 9 were built only for dev and need a refactor before UAT is additive: the Terraform is one root, not a module both roots call, and the pipeline is one file, not templates; and that the seed refuses every deployed environment but dev.

Reconciled when the lead has approved or declined the proposal.
